Optimal Agnostic Control of Unknown Linear Dynamics in a Bounded Parameter Range
J. Carruth, M. F. Eggl, C. Fefferman, C. W. Rowley

Abstract
Here and in the follow-on paper [ 6 ] , we consider a simple control problem in which the underlying dynamics depend on a parameter a a that is unknown and must be learned. In this paper, we assume that a a is bounded, i.e., that | a | ≤ a MAX |a|\leq a_{\text{MAX}} , and we study two variants of the control problem. In the first variant, Bayesian control, we are given a prior probability distribution for a a and we seek a strategy that minimizes the expected value of a given cost function. Assuming that we can solve a certain PDE (the Hamilton-Jacobi-Bellman equation), we produce optimal strategies for Bayesian control. In the second variant, agnostic control, we assume nothing about a a and we seek a strategy that minimizes a quantity called the regret. We produce a prior probability distribution d ​ Prior ​ ( a ) d\text{Prior}(a) supported on a finite subset of [ − a MAX , a MAX ] [-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}] so that the agnostic control problem reduces to the Bayesian control problem for the prior d ​ Prior ​ ( a ) d\text{Prior}(a) .



1
Introduction

Here and in
[
7
,
16
]
, we explore a new flavor of adaptive control theory, which we call ‘‘agnostic control.’’
0
0
footnotetext:
This work was supported by AFOSR grant FA9550-19-1-0005 and by the Joachim Herz Foundation.
Our introduction borrows heavily from that of
[
6
]
.

Many works in adaptive control theory attempt to control a system whose underlying dynamics are initially unknown and must be learned from observation. The goal is then to bound
Regret
, a quantity defined by comparing our expected cost with that incurred by an opponent who knows the underlying dynamics. Typically one tries to achieve a regret whose order of magnitude is as small as possible after a long time. Adaptive control theory has extensive practical applications; see, e.g.,
[
4
,
8
,
21
,
29
]
.

In some applications, we don’t have the luxury of waiting for a long time. This is the case, e.g., for a pilot attempting to land an airplane following the sudden loss of a wing, as in
[
5
]
. Our goal, here and in
[
6
]
, is to achieve the absolute minimum possible regret over a fixed, finite time horizon. This poses formidable mathematical challenges, even for simple model systems.

We will study a one-dimensional, linear model system whose dynamics depend on a single unknown parameter
a
. When
a
is large positive, the system is highly unstable. (There is no “stabilizing gain” for all
a
.) Here, we suppose that the unknown
a
is confined to a known interval
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
and we don’t assume that we are given a Bayesian prior probability distribution for it. In
[
6
]
, we extend our results to deal with the case in which
a
may be any real number.

Modulo an arbitrarily small increase in regret, we reduce the problem, here and in
[
6
]
, to a Bayesian variant in which the unknown
a
is confined to a finite set and governed by a prior probability distribution.

For the Bayesian problem, our task is to find a strategy that minimizes the expected cost. This leads naturally to a PDE, the Bellman equation. We prove here that the optimal strategy for Bayesian control is indeed given in terms of the solution of the Bellman equation, and that any strategy significantly different from that optimum incurs a significantly higher cost. We proceed modulo assumptions about existence and regularity of the relevant PDE solutions, for which we lack rigorous proofs. (However, we have obtained numerical solutions, which seem to behave as expected.)

Let us now explain the above in more detail.

The Model System

Our system consists of a particle moving in one dimension, influenced by our control and buffeted by noise. The position of our particle at time
t
is denoted by
q(t)\in\mathbb{R}
. At each time
t
, we may specify a “control”
u(t)\in\mathbb{R}
, determined by history up to time
t
, i.e., by
(q(s))_{s\in[0,t]}
. A “strategy” (aka “policy”) is a rule for specifying
u(t)
in terms of
(q(s))_{s\in[0,t]}
for each
t
. We write
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\sigma^{*},\text{etc.}
to denote strategies. The noise is provided by a standard Brownian motion
(W(t))_{t\geq 0}
.

The particle moves according to the stochastic ODE

dq(t)=\big(aq(t)+u(t)\big)dt+dW(t),\qquad q(0)=q_{0},

(1.1)

where
a
and
q_{0}
are real parameters. Due to the noise in (
1.1
),
q(t)
and
u(t)
are random variables; these random variables depend on our strategy
\sigma
, and we often write
q^{\sigma}(t)
,
u^{\sigma}(t)
to make that dependence explicit.

Over a time horizon
T>0
, we incur a
Cost
, given
2
2

2

By rescaling, we can consider seemingly different cost functions of the form
\int_{0}^{T}(q^{2}+\lambda u^{2})
for
\lambda>0
.
by

\textsc{Cost}=\int_{0}^{T}\big\{(q(t))^{2}+(u(t))^{2}\big\}dt.

(1.2)

This quantity is a random variable determined by
a,q_{0},T
and our strategy
\sigma
. Here, the starting position
q_{0}
and time horizon
T
are fixed and known, but we don’t know the parameter
a
.

We would like to keep our cost as low as possible. We write
\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a)
to denote the expected value of the
Cost
(
1.2
) for the given
a
in (
1.1
).

In this paper we study two variants of the above control problem, which we call
Bayesian control
and
agnostic control
.

For Bayesian control
, we are given a prior probability distribution
d\text{Prior}(a)
for the unknown
a
in (
1.1
). We assume that
d\text{Prior}
is supported in an interval
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Our task is to pick the strategy
\sigma
to minimize

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,d\text{Prior})=\int_{-a_{\text{MAX}}}^{a_{\text{MAX}}}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a)d\text{Prior}(a).

(1.3)

For agnostic control
, we are given that
a
belongs to a known interval
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, but we aren’t given a prior probability distribution
d\text{Prior}(a)
, so we can’t define an expected cost by (
1.3
). Instead, our goal will be to minimize
worst-case regret
, defined by comparing the performance of our strategy with that of an opponent who knows the value of
a
and plays optimally. Let
\sigma_{\text{opt}}(a)
be the optimal strategy for known
a
. Thus
\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a)
is minimized over all
\sigma
by taking
\sigma=\sigma_{\text{opt}}(a)
.
1
1

1

See standard textbooks (e.g.,
[
3
]
) for the computation of
\sigma_{\text{opt}}(a)
and its expected cost.
We will introduce several variants of the notion of regret.

To a given strategy
\sigma
we associate the following functions on
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
:

•

Additive Regret
, defined as

\text{AReg}(\sigma,a)=\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a)-\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{\text{opt}}(a),a)\geq 0.

•

Multiplicative Regret
(aka “competitive ratio”), defined as

\text{MReg}(\sigma,a)=\frac{\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a)}{\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{\text{opt}}(a),a)}\geq 1.

•

Hybrid Regret
, defined in terms of a parameter
\gamma>0
by setting

\text{HReg}_{\gamma}(\sigma,a)=\frac{\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a)}{\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{\text{opt}}(a),a)+\gamma}

See
[
6
]
for a discussion of the regimes in which these three notions provide useful information.

Writing
\textsc{Regret}(\sigma,a)
to denote any one of the above three functions on
[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
, we define the
worst-case regret
:

\textsc{Regret}^{*}(\sigma)=\sup\{\textsc{Regret}(\sigma,a):a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]\}.

(1.4)

We seek a strategy
\sigma
having the least possible worst-case regret.

Thus, we have posed two problems: For Bayesian control, find the strategy that minimizes our expected cost; and for agnostic control, find a strategy that minimizes worst-case regret.

To prepare to present our results, we next discuss a relevant PDE, the
Bellman equation
.

We will see that the problem of Bayesian control is intimately connected to the following PDE for an unknown function
S(q,t,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
of four variables:

\begin{split}0=&\partial_{t}S+(\bar{a}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})q+u_{\text{opt}})\partial_{q}S+\bar{a}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})q^{2}\partial_{\zeta_{1}}S+q^{2}\partial_{\zeta_{2}}S+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}^{2}S\\
&+q\partial_{q\zeta_{1}}S+\frac{1}{2}q^{2}\partial_{\zeta_{1}}^{2}S+(q^{2}+u_{\text{opt}}^{2}),\end{split}

(1.5)

where

u_{\text{opt}}=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}S,

(1.6)

with terminal condition

S|_{t=T}=0.

(1.7)

Here,
\bar{a}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
is a known, smooth function of two variables.

We have succeeded in finding numerical solutions of (
1.5
)–(
1.7
), but we lack rigorous proofs of existence and smoothness of solutions. Accordingly, we impose a
PDE Assumption
to the effect that (
1.5
)–(
1.7
) admit a solution
S
satisfying plausible estimates (see Section
3
). Our numerics suggest that the PDE Assumption is correct.
Our results below are conditional on the PDE Assumption
.

We are ready to state our main results. We begin with Bayesian control. For a function
\bar{a}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
given in terms of
d\text{Prior}
by an elementary formula, we define a function
u_{\text{opt}}(q,t,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
as in (
1.5
)–(
1.7
), and then specify a strategy
\sigma=\sigma_{\text{Bayes}}(d\text{Prior})
by setting

\displaystyle u^{\sigma}(t)=u_{\text{opt}}(q^{\sigma}(t),t,\zeta_{1}(t),\zeta_{2}(t)),\;\text{with}

(1.8)

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}q^{\sigma}(s)[dq^{\sigma}(s)-u^{\sigma}(s)ds]\qquad\text{and}\qquad\zeta_{2}(t)=\int_{0}^{t}(q^{\sigma}(s))^{2}\ ds.

(1.9)

Note that
\zeta_{1}(t)
,
\zeta_{2}(t)
are determined by past history up through time
t
, hence so is
u^{\sigma}(t)
in (
1.8
). As explained in
[
6
]
, heuristic reasoning suggests that
\sigma_{\text{Bayes}}(d\text{Prior})
is the optimal strategy for Bayesian control with prior belief
d\text{Prior}
. Our rigorous result confirms this intuition. Recall that
q_{0}
is our starting position.

Theorem 1
.

Fix a probability distribution
d\emph{Prior}
on
[-a_{\emph{MAX}},a_{\emph{MAX}}]
, and let
S
,
u_{\emph{opt}}
,
\sigma=\sigma_{\emph{Bayes}}(d\emph{Prior})
be as above. Then the following hold.

(A)

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,d\emph{Prior})=S(q_{0},0,0,0)
.

(B)

Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be any other strategy. Then

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\prime},d\emph{Prior})\geq\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,d\emph{Prior}).

For a class of “tame strategies”
\sigma^{\prime}
, we can sharpen
(B)
above to a quantitative result. A
tame strategy

\sigma^{\prime}
satisfies the estimate

|u^{\sigma^{\prime}}(t)|\leq\hat{C}[|q^{\sigma^{\prime}}(t)|+1]\;\text{(all }t\in[0,T])

with probability 1, for a constant
\hat{C}
, called a
tame constant for
\sigma^{\prime}
. The quantitative version of
(B)
is as follows.

Theorem 2

(Quantitative Uniqueness)
.

Let
d\emph{Prior}
,
\sigma=\sigma_{\emph{Bayes}}(d\emph{Prior})
be as in Theorem
1
. Given
\varepsilon>0
and given a constant
\hat{C}
, there exists
\delta>0
for which the following holds:

Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be a tame strategy with tame constant
\hat{C}
.

If
\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\prime},d\emph{Prior})\leq\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,d\emph{Prior})+\delta
, then the expected value of

\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\sigma^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t)-u^{\sigma^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt

is less than
\varepsilon
.

Quantitative Uniqueness will play a crucial rôle in our analysis of agnostic control.

Our main result for agnostic control is as follows.

Theorem 3
.

Fix
[-a_{\emph{MAX}},a_{\emph{MAX}}]
,
q_{0}
,
T
(and
\gamma
if we use hybrid regret). Then there exist a probability measure
d\emph{Prior}
, a finite subset
E\subset[-a_{\emph{MAX}},a_{\emph{MAX}}]
, and a strategy
\sigma
, for which the following hold.

(I)

\sigma
is the optimal Bayesian strategy for the prior probability distribution
d\emph{Prior}
.

(II)

d\emph{Prior}
is supported in the finite set
E
.

(III)

E
is precisely the set of points
a\in[-a_{\emph{MAX}},a_{\emph{MAX}}]
at which the function
[-a_{\emph{MAX}},a_{\emph{MAX}}]\ni a\mapsto\textsc{Regret}(\sigma,a)
achieves its maximum.

(IV)

\textsc{Regret}^{*}(\sigma)\leq\textsc{Regret}^{*}(\sigma^{\prime})
for any other strategy
\sigma^{\prime}
.

So, for optimal agnostic control, we should pretend to believe that the unknown
a
is confined to a finite set
E
and governed by the probability distribution
d\text{Prior}
, even though in fact we know nothing about
a
except that it lies in
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
.

Let
d\text{Prior}
,
\sigma
,
E
be as in (I), (II), (III) of Theorem
3
. Since
\sigma
is the optimal Bayesian strategy for
d\text{Prior}
(by (I)), and since
d\text{Prior}
is supported on the finite set
E
(by (II)), we have for any other strategy
\sigma^{\prime}
that

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a_{0})\leq\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\prime},a_{0})\;\text{for some}\;a_{0}\in E.

In particular, we have

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma,a_{0})\leq\textsc{Regret}(\sigma^{\prime},a_{0})\;\text{for some}\;a_{0}\in E.

Combining this with (III), we see that for any
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
we have

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma,a)\leq\textsc{Regret}(\sigma^{\prime},a_{0}).

Therefore (I), (II), (III) of Theorem
3
easily imply (IV). The hard part of Theorem
3
is the assertion that there exist
d\text{Prior}
,
E
,
\sigma
, satisfying (I), (II), (III).

Theorem
3
lets us search for optimal agnostic strategies: We first guess a finite set
E
and a probability measure
d\text{Prior}
concentrated on
E
. By solving the Bellman equation, we produce the optimal Bayesian strategy
\sigma=\sigma_{\text{Bayes}}(d\text{Prior})
, which allows us to compute the function
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]\ni a\mapsto\textsc{Regret}(\sigma,a)
. If the maximum of that function occurs precisely at the points of
E
, then
\sigma
is the desired optimal agnostic strategy. Otherwise, we modify our guess
(E,d\text{Prior})
. We have carried this out numerically for several
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}],q_{0},T
.

This concludes our introductory discussion of agnostic control for bounded
a
(i.e.
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
).

We briefly touch on another variant of the control problem (
1.1
):
agnostic control for unbounded
a
.

Suppose we assume absolutely nothing about our unknown
a
; it might be any real number. For any strategy
\sigma
we define
\textsc{Regret}^{*}(\sigma)
as in (
1.4
), except that now the
\sup
is taken over all
a\in\mathbb{R}
. Our task is to pick
\sigma
to minimize
\textsc{Regret}^{*}(\sigma)
.

Our companion paper
[
6
]
analyzes this problem by comparing optimal agnostic control for arbitrary
a
with the case in which
a
is confined to a large interval
[-a_{\text{MAX}}(\varepsilon),+a_{\text{MAX}}(\varepsilon)]
, depending on a small parameter
\varepsilon>0
. Roughly speaking,
[
6
]
shows that any strategy for
a
confined to
[-a_{\text{MAX}}(\varepsilon),+a_{\text{MAX}}(\varepsilon)]
may be modified to produce a strategy for arbitrary
a\in\mathbb{R}
, with an increase in worst-case hybrid regret of at most
\varepsilon
. (See
[
6
]
for precise statements.)

Recap

Let us summarize what we have achieved. Suppose our goal is to minimize worst-case hybrid regret in the setting in which
a
may be any real number. Modulo an arbitrarily small increase in regret, we may reduce matters to the case in which
a
is confined to a bounded interval
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. We then look for a probability measure
d\text{Prior}
living on a finite set
E\subset[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, such that the regret of the optimal Bayesian strategy for
d\text{Prior}
is maximized precisely on
E
. We can calculate the optimal Bayesian strategy for a given prior probability measure by solving a Bellman equation. However, our results are conditional; we have to make an assumption on the existence, smoothness, and size of solutions to the Bellman equation. In numerical simulations, we have produced evidence for our PDE Assumptions, and we have produced optimal agnostic strategies for cases in which the unknown
a
is confined to an interval.

Ideas from the Proofs

We mention one significant technical point regarding the proofs of Theorems
1
,
2
,
3
: We need a rigorous definition of a strategy. Certainly the phrase “a rule for determining
u(t)
from past history” isn’t precise.

We want to allow
u(t)
to depend discontinuously on past history
(q(s))_{s\in[0,t]}
. For instance, we should be allowed to set

u(t)=\begin{cases}-q(t)&\text{if}\;|q(t)|>1,\\
0&\text{otherwise}.\end{cases}

On the other hand, we had better make sure that we can produce solutions of our stochastic ODE

dq=(aq+u)dt+dW.

We proceed as follows.

At first we fix a partition

0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=T

(1.10)

of the time interval
[0,T]
. We restrict ourselves to strategies
\sigma
in which the control
u(t)
is constant in each interval
[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1})
, and in which, for each
\nu
,
u(t_{\nu})
is determined by
q(t_{1}),\dots,q(t_{\nu})
, together with “coin flips”
\vec{\xi}=(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\cdots)\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. For all
\nu
, we assume that
u(t_{\nu})
is a Borel measurable function of
(q(t_{1}),\dots,q(t_{\nu}),\vec{\xi})
, and that

|u(t_{\nu})|\leq C_{\text{TAME}}[|q(t_{\nu})|+1].

A strategy as above is called a
tame strategy associated to the partition
(
1.10
), with a
tame constant

C_{\text{TAME}}
. For such strategies, it is easy to define the solutions
q^{\sigma}(t)
,
u^{\sigma}(t)
of our stochastic ODE (
1.1
).

Most of our work lies in controlling and optimizing tame strategies associated to a sufficiently fine partition. In particular, we will prove approximate versions of Theorems
1
,
2
in the setting of such strategies.

We will then define a tame strategy (not associated to any partition) by considering a sequence
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\dots
of ever-finer partitions of
[0,T]
. To each partition
\pi_{n}
we associate a tame strategy
\sigma_{n}
with a tame constant
C_{\text{TAME}}
independent of
n
. If the resulting
q^{\sigma_{n}}(t)
and
u^{\sigma_{n}}(t)
tend to limits, in an appropriate sense, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, then we declare these limits
q(t)
,
u(t)
to arise from a
tame strategy

\sigma
with a
tame constant

C_{\text{TAME}}
.

Finally, we drop the restriction to tame strategies and consider general strategies. To do so, we consider a sequence
(\sigma_{n})_{n=0,1,2,\dots}
of tame strategies, NOT assumed to have a tame constant independent of
n
. If the relevant
q^{\sigma_{n}}(t)
and
u^{\sigma_{n}}(t)
converge, in a suitable sense, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, then we say that the limits
q(t)
,
u(t)
arise from a strategy
\sigma
.

It isn’t hard to pass from tame strategies associated to partitions of
[0,T]
to general tame strategies, and then to pass from such tame strategies to general strategies. The work in proving Theorems
1
and
2
lies in our close study of tame strategies associated to fine partitions.

We provide only a few comments on the proof of Theorem
3
. The main work lies in proving an analogue of Theorem
3
in which the unknown
a
is confined to a finite set
A\subset[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, rather than to the whole of
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. We apply that analogue to a sequence
A_{1},A_{2},\dots
of fine nets in
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, e.g.,
A_{n}=[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]\cap 2^{-n}\mathbbm{Z}
, and pass to the limit as
n\rightarrow\infty
using a weak compactness argument. To establish the result for finite
A
, we proceed by induction on the number of elements of
A
. Details may be found in Chapter
5
.

Future Directions

Our work suggests several unsolved problems, among which we mention:

•

Prove (or disprove) the PDE Assumption.

•

Consider problems in which the particle lives in
\mathbb{R}^{N}
, not just in
\mathbb{R}^{1}
; and in which the dynamics of the particle depend on more than one unknown parameter. Can that be done without rendering the relevant numerics hopelessly impractical?

•

Even for the model problem considered in this paper, improve the numerics to let us produce optimal agnostic strategies for a larger range of
a_{\text{MAX}}
and
T
than we can deal with today.

We speculate briefly on a particular model problem in which we don’t know a priori what our control does.

Consider a particle governed by the stochastic ODE

dq(t)=au(t)dt+dW(t),\qquad q(0)=0.

(1.11)

As usual,
q(t)
denotes position,
u(t)
is our control,
W(t)
is Brownian motion, and we incur a cost

\int_{0}^{T}\{(q(t))^{2}+(u(t))^{2}\}\ dt.

In the simplest case, suppose we know a priori that
a=1
or
a=-1
, each with probability
1/2
. We write
\textsc{ECost}(\sigma)
to denote the expected cost incurred by executing a strategy
\sigma
, and we set

\textsc{ECost}^{*}=\inf\{\textsc{ECost}(\sigma):\text{All strategies }\sigma\}.

(1.12)

For this simple model problem we conjecture that the inf in (
1.12
) is not achieved by any strategy
\sigma
, because heuristic reasoning suggests that there is a regime in which we would like to set
u=\pm\infty
to gain instant information about
a
.

Clearly there is much to be done before we can claim to understand agnostic control theory.

Survey of Prior Literature

Literature that considers adaptive control of a simple linear system similar to the one considered in this paper commonly consists of one or more of the following features: (
i
) unknown governing dynamics, (
ii
) unknown cost function and (
iii
) adversarial noise. Examples of such work include
[
24
,
26
,
33
,
18
,
11
,
25
,
14
]
as well as our own prior work
[
16
]
,
[
7
]
.

Initial work in obtaining regret bounds in the infinite time horizon for the related LQR (linear-quadratic regulator) problem was undertaken in
[
1
]
, which proved that under certain assumptions, the expected additive regret of the adaptive controller is bounded by
\tilde{O}(\sqrt{T})
. Further progress was made on this problem in
[
9
]
. Assuming controllability of the system, the authors gave the first efficient algorithm capable of attaining sublinear additive regret in a single trajectory in the setting of online nonstochastic control. See also the related
[
28
]
, which obtained sublinear adaptive regret bounds, a stronger metric than standard regret and more suitable for time-varying systems. Additional adaptive control approaches include
[
12
,
13
]
using the system level synthesis. This expands on ideas in
[
31
]
, which showed that the ordinary least-squares estimator learns a linear system nearly optimally in one shot. Other work uses Thompson Sampling
[
2
,
23
]
or deep learning
[
10
]
. Perhaps most related to the work performed in this study is
[
22
]
, which designed an online learning algorithm with sublinear expected regret that moves away from episodic estimates of the state dynamics (meaning that no boundedness or initially stabilizing control needed to be assumed).

In
[
16
]
, the third and fourth authors of the present paper, along with B. Guillén Pegueroles and M. Weber, found regret minimizing strategies for a problem with simple unknown dynamics (a particle moving in one-dimension at a constant, unknown velocity subject to Brownian motion). In
[
20
]
, along with D. Goswami and D. Gurevich, they generalized these results to an analogous, higher-dimensional system with the addition of sensor noise. In
[
16
]
, they also posed the problem of finding regret minimizing strategies for the more complicated dynamics (
1.1
). In
[
7
]
, the authors of the present paper, along with M. Weber, took the first steps toward resolving this problem. Specifically, we exhibited a strategy for the dynamics (
1.1
) with bounded multiplicative regret.

Historically, significant work has been undertaken in the closely related “multi-armed bandit” problem; see, for instance, the classic papers
[
30
,
32
]
. Recent work considering this paradigm includes
[
34
]
, which used reinforcement learning to obtain dynamic regret whose order of magnitude is optimal, and
[
15
]
, which studied the more general Generalized Linear Bandits (GLBs) and obtained similar regret bounds.

We finally want to point out the parallel field of adversarial control, where the noise profile is chosen by an adversary instead of randomly. This includes
[
27
]
, which attained minimum dynamic regret and guaranteed compliance with hard safety constraints in the face of uncertain disturbance realizations using the system level synthesis framework, and
[
19
]
, which studied the problem of competitive control.

As this list of references is by no means exhaustive and does not do justice to the wealth of studies in the literature, we point the reader to the book
[
21
]
and the references therein for a more thorough overview of online control.

We emphasize that our approach in
[
6
]
,
[
7
]
,
[
16
]
, and the present paper differs from the other work cited above in that:

•

We seek strategies that minimize the worst-case regret for a fixed time horizon
T
, whereas the literature is mainly concerned with
T\rightarrow\infty
.

•

Typically in the literature one assumes either that the dynamics are bounded or that one is given a stabilizing control. We make no such assumptions in
[
6
]
and so we must control a system that is arbitrarily unstable.

•

However, we achieve the above ambitious goals only for a simple model system.

We thank Amir Ali Ahmadi, Brittany Hamfeldt, Elad Hazan, Robert Kohn, Sam Otto, Allan Sly, and Melanie Weber for helpful conversations. We are grateful to the Air Force Office of Scientific Research, especially Frederick Leve, for their generous support. The second named author thanks the Joachim Herz foundation for support.

Chapter 1
The Game

We will deal with random variables

\displaystyle a_{\text{TRUE}}\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}],

(0.1)

\displaystyle\text{``Coin flips'' }\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\dots\in\{0,1\}\text{ (we write }\vec{\xi}\text{ for }(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\dots)),\text{and}

(0.2)

\displaystyle\text{Brownian motion }W(t),\text{ starting at }W(0)=0.

(0.3)

The random variable
a_{\text{TRUE}}
is deterministic and known in Chapter
2
, unknown but subject to a known prior in Chapters
3
and
4
, and unknown without a known prior in Chapter
5
.

The
\xi_{\nu}
, the real number
a_{\text{TRUE}}
, and the Brownian motion are mutually independent. The variable
a_{\text{TRUE}}
has a prior probability distribution given by the measure
d\text{Prior}(a)
in Chapters
3
and
4
; and each
\xi_{\nu}
is equal to 0 with probability
1/2
, and to 1 with probability
1/2
.

When
a_{\text{TRUE}}
has a prior probability distribution, we write
\text{Prob}[\mathcal{E}]
to denote the probability of an event
\mathcal{E}
with respect to the above probability space, and we write
\text{E}[X]
for the expected value of
X
with respect to that probability space.

Given
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
, we write
\text{Prob}_{a}[\mathcal{E}]
for the probability of event
\mathcal{E}
conditioned on the event
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
, and we write
\text{E}_{a}[X]
for the expectation of
X
conditioned on
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
.
\text{Prob}_{a}[\cdots]
and
\text{E}_{a}[\cdots]
make sense without an assumed prior for
a
.

Similarly, given
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
and
\vec{\eta}=(\eta_{1},\eta_{2},\dots)\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we write
\text{Prob}_{a,\vec{\eta}}[\mathcal{E}]
and
\text{E}_{a,\vec{\eta}}[X]
to denote the probability and expectation, respectively, conditioned on the event
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
and
\xi_{\nu}=\eta_{\nu}
for all
\nu
.

Also, we write
\text{E}_{\vec{\eta}}[X]
and
\text{Prob}_{\vec{\eta}}[\mathcal{E}]
to denote expectation and probability, respectively, conditioned on
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
.

The above conditional expectations make sense even if, e.g.,
a
isn’t in the support of
d\text{Prior}
.

Fix a
terminal time

T>0
and a partition

0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=T\;\text{of}\;[0,T].

Fix a
starting position

q_{0}\in\mathbb{R}
. A
tame rule
at time
t_{\nu}
is a Borel measurable function
\sigma_{t_{\nu}}:\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
, satisfying the estimate

|\sigma_{t_{\nu}}(q_{1},\dots,q_{\nu},\vec{\xi})|\leq C_{\text{TAME}}[|q_{\nu}|+1]

(0.4)

for all
(q_{1},\dots,q_{\nu},\vec{\xi})\in\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}.

If
\nu=0
, then
\sigma_{t_{\nu}}
is simply a function on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. (We use the product topology on
\mathbb{R}^{\nu}\times\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
to define Borel measurability. We require Borel measurability to avoid technicalities. In particular, the composition of Borel measurable functions is Borel measurable, whereas the composition of Lebesgue measurable functions needn’t be Lebesgue measurable.)

A
tame strategy
is an array
\sigma=(\sigma_{t_{\nu}})_{\nu=0,1,\dots,N-1}
, where, for each
\nu
,
\sigma_{t_{\nu}}
is a tame rule at time
t_{\nu}
with the same
C_{\text{TAME}}
serving in (
0.4
) for all the
t_{\nu}
. We call
C_{\text{TAME}}
a
tame constant
for the strategy
\sigma
. Until further notice, we say simply “strategy” in place of “tame strategy.” If the
\sigma_{t_{\nu}}
don’t depend on the coin flips
\vec{\xi}
, we call
\sigma
a
deterministic strategy
. We will often write
\sigma_{\nu}
in place of
\sigma_{t_{\nu}}
.

Given a strategy
\sigma=(\sigma_{t_{\nu}})_{\nu=0,1,\dots,N-1}
, we define random variables
q^{\sigma}(t)
for
t\in[0,T]
and
u^{\sigma}(t)
for
t\in[0,T)
, as follows.

By induction on
\nu
, we define
q^{\sigma}(t)
for
t\in[0,t_{\nu}]
and
u^{\sigma}(t)
for
t\in[0,t_{\nu})
.

In the base case
\nu=0
, we set
q^{\sigma}(t)=q_{0}
for
t\in[0,t_{\nu}]=\{0\}
. Since
[0,t_{\nu})=[0,0)
is empty, there is no need to define
u^{\sigma}
in the base case.

For the induction step, we fix
\nu\geq 0
, and assume that we have defined
q^{\sigma}(t)
for
t\in[0,t_{\nu}]
and
u^{\sigma}(t)
for
t\in[0,t_{\nu})
. We extend the definition of
q^{\sigma}(t)
to
t\in[0,t_{\nu+1}]
, and that of
u^{\sigma}(t)
to
t\in[0,t_{\nu+1})
, as follows.

•

For
t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1})
, we set

u^{\sigma}(t)=\sigma_{t_{\nu}}(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),\vec{\xi}).

•

For
t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}]
, we define
q^{\sigma}(t)
as the solution of the stochastic ODE

dq^{\sigma}(t)=(a_{\text{TRUE}}q^{\sigma}(t)+u^{\sigma}(t))dt+dW(t),

with the initial value
q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
already given by our induction hypothesis.

This completes our induction on
\nu
, so we have defined the random variables
q^{\sigma}(t),u^{\sigma}(t)
.

In addition to
q^{\sigma}(t),u^{\sigma}(t)
, we define random variables
\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
,
\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})

(0\leq\nu<N)
by the following induction.

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{0})=\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{0})=0.\quad\text{(Recall, $t_{0}=0$.)}

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})=\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})+q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})\cdot[\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}-u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}],\;\text{where}

\displaystyle\qquad\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}=q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})\;\text{and}\;\Delta t_{\nu}=t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu}.

\displaystyle\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})=\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})+(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}.

Thus,

\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})=\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu})(\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\mu}-u^{\sigma}(t_{\mu})\Delta t_{\mu})

and

\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})=\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}(q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\mu}.

We will try to pick our strategy
\sigma
to make the expected value of

\int_{0}^{T}[(q^{\sigma}(t))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t))^{2}]\ dt

as small as possible.

Chapter 2
Tame Strategies Associated to Partitions

1
Setup

In this chapter, we take
a_{\text{TRUE}}
to be fixed,
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
, and we suppose that our strategy makes no use of coin flips.

We fix a partition

0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=T

(1.1)

of a time interval
[0,T]
.

We fix a (deterministic) strategy
\sigma
for the game with starting position
q_{0}
. We assume that our strategy is tame, i.e.,

|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq C_{\text{TAME}}[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1]

(1.2)

for a constant
C_{\text{TAME}}
.

We write
c
,
C
,
C^{\prime}
, etc. to denote constants determined by

•

C_{\text{TAME}}
in (
1.2
)

•

An upper bound for the time horizon
T

•

An upper bound for
a_{\text{TRUE}}

•

An upper bound for
|q_{0}|
.

These symbols may denote different constants in different occurrences.

We define

\Delta t_{\nu}:=t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu}\;\text{for all}\;\nu\;(0\leq\nu<N)

and we assume that

(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}):=\max_{\nu}\Delta t_{\nu}\;\text{is less than a small enough constant }c.

(1.3)

We write
X=O(Y)
to denote the estimate
|X|\leq CY
.

We write
q_{\nu}
to denote
q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
,
u_{\nu}
to denote
u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
, and
\Delta q_{\nu}
to denote
q_{\nu+1}-q_{\nu}
.

Note that

u_{\nu}=\sigma_{\nu}(q_{1},\dots,q_{\nu}),

(1.4)

where
\sigma_{\nu}
is given by the strategy
\sigma
for decisions at time
t_{\nu}
.

Thanks to (
1.2
), we have

|u_{\nu}|\leq C[|q_{\nu}|+1].

(1.5)

Recall that the
q_{\nu}
evolve as follows.

q_{0}
is given, and
u_{0}
is specified by the strategy
\sigma
.
q_{\nu+1}
and
u_{\nu+1}
are then determined from
q_{\nu}
and
u_{\nu}
as follows.

•

We solve the stochastic ODE

dq(t)=(aq(t)+u_{\nu})dt+dW(t)\qquad\text{for }t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}],

(1.6)

with initial condition
q(t_{\nu})=q_{\nu}
. Here,
a
is the (given) value of
a_{\text{TRUE}}
, and
W(t)
denotes Brownian motion at time
t
.

•

We set
q_{\nu+1}=q(t_{\nu+1})
.

•

We set
u_{\nu+1}=\sigma_{\nu+1}(q_{1},\dots,q_{\nu+1})
(compare with (
1.4
)).

Thus, the
q_{\nu}
,
u_{\nu}
are random variables defined by induction on
\nu
.

Solving the ODE (
1.6
) using an integrating factor, we find that

q(t)-q(t_{\nu})=(aq_{\nu}+u_{\nu})\bigg[\frac{e^{a(t-t_{\nu})}-1}{a}\bigg]+\int_{t_{\nu}}^{t}e^{a(t-s)}\ dW(s)

(1.7)

for
t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}]
.

In particular,

\Delta q_{\nu}=q_{\nu+1}-q_{\nu}=(aq_{\nu}+u_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}+\Delta W_{\nu},

(1.8)

where

\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}=\bigg[\frac{e^{a\Delta t_{\nu}}-1}{a}\bigg]

(1.9)

and

\Delta W_{\nu}=\int_{t_{\nu}}^{t_{\nu+1}}e^{a(t_{\nu+1}-s)}\ dW(s).

(1.10)

(If
a=0
, we interpret the above fractions in square brackets as
(t-t_{\nu})
in (
1.7
), and
\Delta t_{\nu}
in (
1.9
).) We warn the reader that
\Delta W_{\nu}\neq W(t_{\nu+1})-W(t_{\nu})
.

Note that
\Delta W_{\nu}
is a normal random variable with mean 0 and variance

\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}=\bigg[\frac{e^{2a\Delta t_{\nu}}-1}{2a}\bigg]

(1.11)

(again, equal to
\Delta t_{\nu}
if
a=0
).

Note that

\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu},\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}=\Delta t_{\nu}+O((\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}).

(1.12)

Note also that
\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}
,
\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}
,
\Delta W_{\nu}
depend on
a
.

We introduce the sigma algebras
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
, defined as the algebra of events determined by the
\Delta W_{\mu}
for
0\leq\mu<\nu
. Note that
q_{\nu}
,
u_{\nu}
and
\Delta W_{\mu}
(
\mu<\nu
) are
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
-measurable (i.e., they are deterministic once we condition on
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
), while the
\Delta W_{\mu}
for
\mu\geq\nu
are independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
.

Remark 1
.

Thanks to equation (
1.8
), the sigma algebra
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
may be equivalently defined to consist of all events determined by by
q_{1},\dots,q_{\nu}
. This equivalence holds because
a_{\emph{TRUE}}
has been fixed
(a_{\emph{TRUE}}=a)
.

2
Estimates for probabilities of outliers

We suppose

Q>C\;\text{for a large enough}\;C,

(2.1)

and we estimate the probability that
\max_{\nu}|q_{\nu}|>Q
. To do so, we set

\displaystyle u_{\nu}^{1}=u_{\nu}/q_{\nu}\;\text{and}\;u_{\nu}^{0}=0\;\text{if}\;|q_{\nu}|>1;

\displaystyle u_{\nu}^{1}=0\;\text{and}\;u_{\nu}^{0}=u_{\nu}\;\text{otherwise}.

Thus,

u_{\nu}=u_{\nu}^{1}q_{\nu}+u_{\nu}^{0}

(2.2)

and

|u_{\nu}^{1}|,|u_{\nu}^{0}|\leq C.

(2.3)

Also,
u_{\nu}^{1}
and
u_{\nu}^{0}
are
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
-measurable.

Thanks to (
2.2
), we can rewrite (
1.8
) in the form

\Delta q_{\nu}=(a+u_{\nu}^{1})q_{\nu}(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})+u_{\nu}^{0}(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})+\Delta W_{\nu},

or equivalently,

[e^{-at_{\nu+1}}q_{\nu+1}]=(1+e^{-a\Delta t_{\nu}}\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}u_{\nu}^{1})[e^{-at_{\nu}}q_{\nu}]+e^{-at_{\nu+1}}\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}u_{\nu}^{0}+e^{-at_{\nu+1}}\Delta W_{\nu}.

(See (
1.9
).)

Setting

\displaystyle M_{\nu}=\prod_{0\leq\mu<\nu}(1+e^{-a\Delta t_{\mu}}\Delta t_{\mu}^{*}u_{\mu}^{1})^{-1},

(2.4)

\displaystyle m_{\nu}=e^{-at_{\nu+1}}\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}u_{\nu}^{0},\;\text{and}

(2.5)

\displaystyle q_{\nu}^{*}=M_{\nu}e^{-at_{\nu}}q_{\nu},

(2.6)

we see that

\displaystyle q_{\nu+1}^{*}=q_{\nu}^{*}+M_{\nu+1}m_{\nu}+M_{\nu+1}e^{-at_{\nu+1}}\Delta W_{\nu},

(2.7)

and that

\displaystyle q_{0}^{*}=q_{0}.

(2.8)

Since
|e^{-a\Delta t_{\mu}}u_{\mu}^{i}|\leq C\;(i=0,1)
and

\sum_{\mu}(\Delta t_{\mu}^{*})\leq C,

we have

c<M_{\nu}<C\;\text{and}\;|m_{\nu}|<C\Delta t_{\nu}.

(2.9)

Moreover,
M_{\nu+1}
is
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
-measurable.

Let
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
, to be fixed below. From (
2.7
), (
2.9
), we have

\exp(\lambda q_{\nu+1}^{*})\leq\{\exp(\lambda q_{\nu}^{*})\cdot\exp(C|\lambda|\Delta t_{\nu})\}\exp(\{M_{\nu+1}e^{-at_{\nu+1}}\}\lambda\Delta W_{\nu}).

(2.10)

Here, the quantities in curly brackets are
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
-measurable, while
\Delta W_{\nu}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
.

Recalling that
\Delta W_{\nu}
is normal, with mean 0 and variance
O(\Delta t_{\nu})
, we deduce from (
2.9
), (
2.10
) that

\text{E}[\exp(\lambda q_{\nu+1}^{*})|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq\exp(\lambda q_{\nu}^{*})\exp(C|\lambda|\Delta t_{\nu})\exp(C\lambda^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}).

Thus, the random variables

Z_{\nu}=\exp(-C[|\lambda|+\lambda^{2}]t_{\nu})\exp(\lambda q_{\nu}^{*})\quad(0\leq\nu\leq N)

(2.11)

form a supermartingale, with

Z_{0}=\exp(\lambda q_{0})\leq\exp(C|\lambda|).

Consequently, for any
Q>0
we have

\text{Prob}[\max_{\nu}Z_{\nu}>\exp(|\lambda|Q)]\leq\exp(|\lambda|(C-Q)).

By definition (
2.11
), this means that

\text{Prob}[\lambda q_{\nu}^{*}-C[|\lambda|+\lambda^{2}]t_{\nu}>|\lambda|Q\;\text{for some}\;\nu]\leq\exp(|\lambda|(C-Q)).

(2.12)

Taking
Q
greater that
2C
in (
2.12
) (see (
2.1
)), and picking
\lambda=\pm Q
, we learn from (
2.12
) that

\text{Prob}[|q_{\nu}^{*}|>CQ\;\text{for some}\;\nu]\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2}).

Recalling (
2.6
) and (
2.9
), we conclude that

\text{Prob}[\max_{\nu}|q_{\nu}|>Q]\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2})

(2.13)

if
Q
satisfies (
2.1
).

Thus, we have succeeded in estimating the probability that
\max_{\nu}|q_{\nu}|
is large.

Immediately from (
1.5
) and (
2.13
) we have also

\text{Prob}[\max_{\nu}|u_{\nu}|>Q]\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2})

(2.14)

if
Q
satisfies (
2.1
).

We now turn our attention to

\zeta_{1}(t_{\nu})=\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}q_{\mu}[\Delta q_{\mu}-u_{\mu}\Delta t_{\mu}]

(2.15)

and

\zeta_{2}(t_{\nu})=\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}q_{\mu}^{2}\Delta t_{\mu}.

(2.16)

Note that
\zeta_{1}(t_{\nu})
can be rewritten as

\begin{split}\zeta_{1}(t_{\nu})=&\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}\Big\{\frac{1}{2}(q_{\mu+1}^{2}-q_{\mu}^{2})-\frac{1}{2}(\Delta q_{\mu})^{2}\Big\}-\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}u_{\mu}q_{\mu}\Delta t_{\mu}\\
=&\frac{1}{2}q_{\nu}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}q_{0}^{2}-\frac{1}{2}\sum_{\mu=0}^{\nu-1}(\Delta q_{\mu})^{2}-\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}u_{\mu}q_{\mu}\Delta t_{\mu}.\end{split}

(2.17)

Now suppose that

\max_{\nu}|q_{\nu}|,\max_{\nu}|u_{\nu}|\leq CQ\;\text{with}\;Q\;\text{as in \eqref{eq: 1.12}}.

(2.18)

Then from (
2.16
), (
2.17
), we have

|\zeta_{2}(t_{\nu})|\leq CQ^{2}\quad\text{(all }\nu),

(2.19)

and

|\zeta_{1}(t_{\nu})|\leq CQ^{2}+\Big|\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}\{(\Delta q_{\mu})^{2}-\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu}\}\Big|\quad\text{(all }\nu),

(2.20)

since also

\sum_{\mu=0}^{N}(\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu})\leq C\sum_{\mu=0}^{N}\Delta t_{\mu}\leq C^{\prime}.

We will show that

\text{Prob}\Big[\max_{\nu}\Big|\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}\big\{(\Delta q_{\mu})^{2}-\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu}\big\}\Big|>CQ^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big]\\
\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2}).

(2.21)

In view of (
2.13
), (
2.14
), and (
2.21
), estimates (
2.19
), (
2.20
) imply the inequalities

\text{Prob}[\max_{\nu}|\zeta_{1}(t_{\nu})|>CQ^{2}]\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2})

(2.22)

and

\text{Prob}[\max_{\nu}|\zeta_{2}(t_{\nu})|>CQ^{2}]\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2})

(2.23)

for
Q
as in (
2.1
). Thus, to prove (
2.22
) and (
2.23
), it remains only to prove (
2.21
). Estimate (
2.21
) will have further applications in a later section.

We now prove (
2.21
).

From (
1.8
), (
1.12
) we have

\begin{split}\sum_{\mu<\nu}\{(\Delta q_{\mu})^{2}-\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu}\}=&\sum_{\mu<\nu}(aq_{\mu}+u_{\mu})^{2}(\Delta t_{\mu}^{*})^{2}\\
&+2\sum_{\mu<\nu}(aq_{\mu}+u_{\mu})(\Delta t_{\mu}^{*})\Delta W_{\mu}\\
&+\sum_{\mu<\nu}\{(\Delta W_{\mu})^{2}-\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu}\}\\
\equiv&\text{TERM 1}(\nu)+\text{TERM 2}(\nu)+\text{TERM 3}(\nu),\end{split}

(2.24)

with

0\leq\text{TERM 1}(\nu)\leq C\max_{\mu}\{|q_{\mu}|+|u_{\mu}|\}^{2}\cdot(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}),\;\text{all }\nu.

(2.25)

To estimate
\text{TERM 2}(\nu)
, we fix
Q>C
as in (
2.1
) and study the random variables

Y_{\nu}=\exp(-\hat{C}\lambda^{2}Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{2}t_{\nu})\\
\cdot\exp\Big(\lambda\sum_{\mu<\nu}(aq_{\mu}+u_{\mu})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|aq_{\mu}+u_{\mu}|<CQ}(\Delta t_{\mu}^{*})\Delta W_{\mu}\Big)

(2.26)

for a large enough constant
\hat{C}
, and for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
to be picked below. Since
(aq_{\mu}+u_{\mu})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|aq_{\mu}+u_{\mu}|<CQ}
is
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
-measurable for
\mu\leq\nu
, we have

\begin{split}\text{E}[Y_{\nu+1}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=&Y_{\nu}\cdot\exp(-\hat{C}\lambda^{2}Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{2}\Delta t_{\nu})\cdot\\
&\text{E}[\exp([\lambda(aq_{\nu}+u_{\nu})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|aq_{\nu}+u_{\nu}|<CQ}\cdot(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})]\Delta W_{\nu}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\\
\leq&Y_{\nu}\cdot\exp(-\hat{C}\lambda^{2}Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{2}\Delta t_{\nu})\\
&\cdot\exp(C[\lambda(aq_{\nu}+u_{\nu})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|aq_{\nu}+u_{\nu}|<CQ}\cdot(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})]^{2}\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}).\end{split}

(2.27)

(Here, we use the fact that
\Delta W_{\nu}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
, and normal with mean 0 and variance
\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}
.)

If we take
\hat{C}
large enough, then the product of the exponentials on the right in (
2.27
) is less than 1. Thus,

\text{E}[Y_{\nu+1}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq Y_{\nu},\;\text{with}\;Y_{0}\equiv 1.

So the
Y_{\nu}
form a supermartingale. Consequently,

\text{Prob}(\exists\nu\;\text{s.t.}\;Y_{\nu}>\exp(cQ^{2}))\leq\exp(-cQ^{2}).

(2.28)

We now pick
\lambda=[\text{sgn}]\tilde{c}[(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})]^{-1}
with
\text{sgn}=\pm 1
and
\tilde{c}>0
a small enough constant. Combining (
2.26
) and (
2.28
) then yields the estimate

\text{Prob}\Big(\exists\nu\;\text{s.t}\;[(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})]^{-1}\Big|\sum_{\mu<\nu}(aq_{\mu}+u_{\mu})\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|aq_{\mu}+u_{\mu}|<CQ}(\Delta t_{\mu}^{*})\Delta W_{\mu}\Big|>CQ^{2}\Big)\\
\leq\exp(-cQ^{2}).

Consequently,

\text{Prob}\Big(\exists\nu\;\text{s.t.}\;\Big|\sum_{\mu<\nu}(aq_{\mu}+u_{\mu})(\Delta t_{\mu}^{*})\Delta W_{\mu}\Big|>CQ^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})\Big)\\
\leq\exp(-cQ^{2})+\text{Prob}(\exists\mu\;\text{s.t.}\;|aq_{\mu}+u_{\mu}|>CQ).

Recalling (
2.24
), we have

\text{Prob}(\max_{\nu}|\text{TERM 2}(\nu)|>CQ^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}))\\
\leq\exp(-cQ^{2})+\text{Prob}(\max_{\mu}\{|q_{\mu}|+|u_{\mu}|\}>cQ).

(2.29)

We turn our attention to
\text{TERM 3}(\nu)
. Let

\mathcal{Z}_{\nu}=\exp(-\hat{C}\lambda^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})t_{\nu})\cdot\exp\bigg(\lambda\sum_{\mu<\nu}\{(\Delta W_{\mu})^{2}-\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu}\}\bigg)

(2.30)

for a large enough constant
\hat{C}
, and for
\lambda\in\mathbb{R}
to be picked below.

Then
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\equiv 1
, and

\text{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{\nu+1}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=\mathcal{Z}_{\nu}\exp(-\hat{C}\lambda^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})\Delta t_{\nu})\cdot\text{E}[\exp(\lambda\{(\Delta W_{\nu})^{2}-\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}\})].

(2.31)

For
|\lambda|<c(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{-1}
, we have

\begin{split}\text{E}[\exp(\lambda\{(\Delta W_{\nu})^{2}-&\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}\})]\\
&=\exp(-\lambda\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu})\cdot\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{\lambda x^{2}}e^{-x^{2}/2\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}\ dx\\
&=\exp(-\lambda\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu})\cdot(1-2\lambda(\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}))^{-1/2}\\
&\leq\exp(C\lambda^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2})\leq\exp(C\lambda^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})\Delta t_{\nu}).\end{split}

(2.32)

Substituting (
2.32
) into (
2.31
), and taking
\hat{C}
large enough, we find that

\text{E}[\mathcal{Z}_{\nu+1}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq\mathcal{Z}_{\nu},

i.e., the
\mathcal{Z}_{\nu}
form a supermartingale. This holds for
|\lambda|<c(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{-1}
. Since
\mathcal{Z}_{0}\equiv 1
, it follows that

\text{Prob}(\exists\nu\;\text{s.t.}\;\mathcal{Z}_{\nu}>\exp(cQ^{2}))\leq\exp(-cQ^{2}).

Taking
\lambda=(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{-1/2}\cdot[\text{sgn}]
with
\text{sgn}=\pm 1
, we conclude that

\text{Prob}\Big(\exists\nu\;\text{s.t.}\;\exp\Big(-\hat{C}t_{\nu}+(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{-1/2}\Big|\sum_{\mu<\nu}\{(\Delta W_{\mu})^{2}-\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu}\}\Big|\Big)>\exp(cQ^{2})\Big)\\
\leq\exp(-cQ^{2}),

so that

\text{Prob}\Big(\max_{\nu}\Big|\sum_{\mu<\nu}\{(\Delta W_{\mu})^{2}-\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu}\}\Big|>CQ^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big)\leq\exp(-cQ^{2}).

Recalling (
2.24
), we conclude that

\text{Prob}(\max_{\nu}|\text{TERM 3}(\nu)|>CQ^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}))\leq\exp(-cQ^{2}).

(2.33)

Estimates (
2.25
), (
2.29
), (
2.33
) control TERM 1(
\nu
), TERM 2
(\nu)
, TERM 3(
\nu
). Substituting these estimates into (
2.24
), we learn that

\text{Prob}\Big(\max_{\nu}\Big|\sum_{\mu<\nu}\{(\Delta q_{\mu})^{2}-\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu}\}\Big|>C^{\prime}Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big)\\
\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2})+C\text{Prob}(\max_{\mu}\{|q_{\mu}|+|u_{\mu}|\}>cQ).

Finally, recalling (
2.13
) and (
2.14
), we see that

\text{Prob}\Big(\max_{\nu}\Big|\sum_{\mu<\nu}\{(\Delta q_{\mu})^{2}-\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu}\}\Big|>C^{\prime\prime}Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big)\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2}),

completing the proof of (
2.21
).

Next, we estimate

\displaystyle\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}=q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}=\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}=\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}).

Recall that

\displaystyle\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}=(aq^{\sigma}_{\nu}+u^{\sigma}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}+\Delta W_{\nu},

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}=q^{\sigma}_{\nu}(\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}-u^{\sigma}_{\nu}\Delta t_{\nu}),

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}=(q^{\sigma}_{\nu})^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}.

Let
Q\geq C
for large enough
C
, and suppose
|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq Q.
Then also
|u^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq C[|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+1]\leq C^{\prime}Q
, so

\displaystyle|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq CQ(\Delta t_{\nu})+|\Delta W_{\nu}|,

\displaystyle|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|\leq Q(|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+CQ\Delta t_{\nu})\leq C^{\prime}Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})+Q|\Delta W_{\nu}|,\;\text{and}

\displaystyle|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|\leq Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu}),

hence for
p\geq 1
, we have

(|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|+|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|)^{p}\leq C_{p}Q^{2p}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{p}+C_{p}Q^{p}|\Delta W_{\nu}|^{p}.

Recall that
\Delta W_{\nu}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
and normal, with mean 0 and variance at most
C\Delta t_{\nu}
. it follows that, for any
p\geq 1
, we have

\text{E}[(|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|+|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|)^{p}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq C_{p}Q^{2p}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{p}+C_{p}Q^{p}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{p/2}

(2.34)

whenever
|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq Q
. (Recall,
q^{\sigma}_{\nu}
is deterministic once we condition on
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
.) In particular, (
2.34
) implies that

\text{Prob}[|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|+|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|>(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq C(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1000}

(2.35)

if
|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq Q
and
C\leq Q\leq(\Delta t_{\nu})^{-1/1000}
.

Together with (
2.34
) for
p=2,4
and Cauchy-Schwartz, (
2.35
) implies the estimate

\text{E}[(|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|+|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|)^{p}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|+|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|>(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5}}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\\
\leq C(\Delta t_{\nu})^{100}\;\text{for}\;p=1,2,

provided
|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq Q
and
C^{\prime}\leq Q\leq(\Delta t_{\nu})^{-1/1000}
for large enough
C^{\prime}
.

Next, we estimate
|q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|=|q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|
for
t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}]
.

Recall that

q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\sigma}_{\nu}=(aq^{\sigma}_{\nu}+u^{\sigma}_{\nu})\cdot\bigg[\frac{e^{a(t-t_{\nu})}-1}{a}\bigg]+\int_{t_{\nu}}^{t}e^{a(t-s)}\ dW(s)

for
t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}]
.

If
|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq Q
with
Q\geq C
(for large enough
C
), then also
|u^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq CQ
, hence

|q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq CQ(\Delta t_{\nu})+\bigg|\int_{t_{\nu}}^{t}e^{a(t-s)}\ dW(s)\bigg|

for
t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}]
.

Applying the reflection principle
[
17
]
to the Gaussian process

W^{\#}(t)=\int_{t_{\nu}}^{t}e^{-as}\ dW(s)\qquad(t\geq t_{\nu}),

we see that

\text{Prob}\Big[\max_{t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}]}\Big|\int_{t_{\nu}}^{t}e^{a(t-s)}\ dW(s)\Big|>CQ(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1/2}\Big]\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2}).

Since
\int_{t_{\nu}}^{t}e^{a(t-s)}\ dW(s)

(t\geq t_{\nu})
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
, it now follows that

\text{Prob}\Big[\max_{t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}]}|q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|>C^{\prime}Q(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1/2}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}\Big]\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2})

provided
|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq Q
and
C\leq Q
for large enough
C
. Taking

Q=(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5}/[C^{\prime}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1/2}],

we find that

\text{Prob}\Big[\max_{t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}]}|q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|>(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5}\Big|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}\Big]\leq C(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1000}

(2.36)

provided
|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq c\cdot(\Delta t_{\nu})^{-1/10}
.

Let us summarize the results of the above discussion.

Lemma 1

(Lemma on Rare Events)
.

We condition on
a_{\emph{TRUE}}=a
,
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
. Fix a strategy
\sigma
. For constants
c,C
depending only on upper bounds for
|q_{0}|
,
a_{\emph{MAX}}
,
C_{\emph{TAME}}
, and
T
, the following holds.

Suppose
\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}}\equiv\max_{\nu}(t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu})<c
.

Then, for
Q>C
, the following hold with probability
>1-\exp(-cQ^{2})
:

•

|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|,|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq Q
for all
\nu

•

|\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|,|\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq Q^{2}
for all
\nu

•

\big|\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}(q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu+1})-q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}-t_{\nu}\big|\leq Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}})^{1/2}
for all
\nu
.

Moreover, suppose we fix
\nu
and condition on
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
, the sigma algebra of events determined by the
q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu})

(0\leq\mu\leq\nu)
. Suppose that
|q_{\nu}|<(\Delta t_{\nu})^{-1/1000}
. Then

\emph{E}[(|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|+|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|)^{p}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|+|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|>(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5}}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq C(\Delta t_{\nu})^{100}

for
p=1,2
, and

\emph{Prob}[|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|+|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|>(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq C(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1000}.

Also, we have

\emph{Prob}\Big[\max_{t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}]}|q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|>(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}\Big]\leq C(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1000}

provided
|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq c\cdot(\Delta t_{\nu})^{-1/10}
. Finally, for
Q\geq C
, we have

\emph{Prob}\Big[\max_{t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1}]}|q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|>C^{\prime}Q(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1/2}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}\Big]\leq C\exp(-cQ^{2})\;\text{if}\;|q_{\nu}|\leq Q.

Proof.

To deduce the third bullet point from (
2.21
), we note that

\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}(\Delta\tilde{t}_{\mu})=\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}[(\Delta t_{\mu})+O((\Delta t_{\mu})^{2})]=t_{\nu}\cdot(1+O(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})).

The remaining assertions of the lemma have already been proved as stated.
∎

3
The Probability Density

We continue to adopt the assumptions and notation of Section
2
. Our goal is to derive an approximate formula for the joint probability density of
(q_{1},\dots,q_{\bar{N}})=(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{\bar{N}}))
for fixed
{\bar{N}}\leq N
. Let us denote this joint probability density by
\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\bar{N}}).
Thus,

\text{Prob}((q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{\bar{N}}))\in E)=\int_{E}\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\bar{N}})\ d\bar{q}_{1}\cdots d\bar{q}_{\bar{N}}

(3.1)

for measurable sets
E\subset\mathbb{R}^{\bar{N}}
.

By formula (
1.8
),

\Delta q_{\nu}=(aq_{\nu}+u_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}+\Delta W_{\nu}

with
\Delta W_{\nu}
mutually independent and normal, with mean 0 and variance
\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}
; consequently, the joint probability
\Phi
is given by

\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\bar{N}})=\prod_{\nu=0}^{{\bar{N}}-1}\phi_{\nu}

(3.2)

with

\phi_{\nu}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}}\exp\bigg(-\frac{1}{2\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}]^{2}\bigg).

(3.3)

Here,
\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}\equiv\bar{q}_{\nu+1}-\bar{q}_{\nu}
,
\bar{q}_{0}\equiv q_{0}
, and
\bar{u}_{\nu}
denotes the control exercised by the strategy
\sigma
at time
t_{\nu}
given that
q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu})=\bar{q}_{\mu}
for
0\leq\mu\leq\nu
and
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
. Note that
\bar{u}_{\nu}
is determined by
\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\nu}
(and
q_{0})
.

We make the following assumptions on
(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\bar{N}})
:

\displaystyle\max_{\nu}(|\bar{q}_{\nu}|+|\bar{u}_{\nu}|)\leq Q,

(3.4)

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}-t_{\bar{N}}\Big|\leq Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/4},

(3.5)

with
Q\geq C
given.

Thanks to Lemma
1
, (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) are very likely true for
(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\bar{N}})=(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{\bar{N}})).
Under assumptions (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) we will simplify (
3.2
), (
3.3
).

First of all, since
\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}=\Delta t_{\nu}+O((\Delta t_{\nu})^{2})
we have

[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}]=[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]+\text{ERR}_{\nu},

with

\text{ERR}_{\nu}=O(Q(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2})

thanks to (
3.4
).

Hence,

\displaystyle[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}]^{2}=

\displaystyle[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}+\text{ERR}_{\nu}^{2}

\displaystyle+2\text{ERR}_{\nu}[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}+O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{3})

\displaystyle+2\text{ERR}_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}).

Therefore,

\begin{split}\sum_{\nu}\frac{1}{2\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-&(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}]^{2}\\
=&\sum_{\nu}\frac{1}{2\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}+\sum_{\nu}O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2})\\
&+\sum_{\nu}\frac{\text{ERR}_{\nu}}{\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}).\end{split}

(3.6)

The last sum has absolute value at most

C\sum_{\nu}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{-1/2}\bigg\{\frac{\text{ERR}_{\nu}}{\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}\bigg\}^{2}+C\sum_{\nu}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1/2}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}.

(3.7)

The expression (
3.7
) is, in turn, at most

\displaystyle\sum_{\nu}O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{3/2})+

\displaystyle C(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\sum_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2})+C(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big|\sum_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}-t_{\bar{N}}\Big|

\displaystyle+Ct_{\bar{N}}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}),

thanks to (
3.5
). Therefore, (
3.6
) implies that

\sum_{\nu}\frac{1}{2\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}]^{2}\\
=\sum_{\nu}\frac{1}{2\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}+O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}).

(3.8)

We want to replace
\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}
by
\Delta t_{\nu}
on the right in (
3.8
). To do so, note that

\frac{1}{2\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}-\frac{1}{2\Delta t_{\nu}}=-\frac{a}{2}+O(\Delta t_{\nu}),

thanks to (
1.11
). Consequently,

\begin{split}\sum_{\nu}\Big(\frac{1}{2\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}-\frac{1}{2\Delta t_{\nu}}\Big)[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}&-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}\\
=&\sum_{\nu}[a+O(\Delta t_{\nu})](a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})\\
&+\sum_{\nu}\Big[-\frac{a}{2}+O(\Delta t_{\nu})\Big](\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}\\
&+\sum_{\nu}\Big[-\frac{a}{2}+O(\Delta t_{\nu})\Big](a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}\\
\equiv&\text{Term}\;\alpha+\text{Term}\;\beta+\text{Term}\;\gamma\end{split}

(3.9)

Now

\displaystyle\text{Term}\;\beta

\displaystyle=-\frac{a}{2}\sum_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}+O(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})\sum_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}

\displaystyle=-\frac{a}{2}t_{\bar{N}}+O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/4})

by (
3.5
), while

\text{Term}\;\gamma=\sum_{\nu}O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2})=O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}))

by (
3.4
). To estimate
\text{Term}\;\alpha
, we apply (
3.4
) and (
3.5
) to write:

\displaystyle|\text{Term}\;\alpha|\leq

\displaystyle C\sum_{\nu}(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{3/2}+C\sum_{\nu}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1/2}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2})+C(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\sum_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2})+C(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big[\sum_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}-t_{\bar{N}}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}).

Combining our estimates for Terms
\alpha,\beta,\gamma
, and recalling (
3.9
), we learn that

\sum_{\nu}\Big(\frac{1}{2\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}-\frac{1}{2\Delta t_{\nu}}\Big)[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}=-\frac{a}{2}t_{\bar{N}}+O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/4}).

Consequently, (
3.8
) implies that

\sum_{\nu}\frac{1}{2\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}]^{2}\\
=-\frac{a}{2}t_{\bar{N}}+\sum_{\nu}\frac{1}{2\Delta t_{\nu}}[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}+O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/4}).

(3.10)

Again applying (
1.11
), we see that

\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta t_{\nu}}}\cdot(1-\frac{1}{2}a\Delta t_{\nu}+O(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2})\\
=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta t_{\nu}}}\exp(-\frac{1}{2}a\Delta t_{\nu}+O(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2})

so that

\begin{split}\prod_{\nu=0}^{{\bar{N}}-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}}}=&\bigg(\prod_{\nu=0}^{{\bar{N}}-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta t_{\nu}}}\bigg)\exp\Big(-\frac{a}{2}\sum_{\nu}\{(\Delta t_{\nu})+O(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}\}\Big)\\
=&\bigg(\prod_{\nu=0}^{{\bar{N}}-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta t_{\nu}}}\bigg)\exp\Big(-\frac{a}{2}t_{\bar{N}}+O(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})\Big).\end{split}

(3.11)

Putting (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) into (
3.2
) and (
3.3
), we find that

\begin{split}\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\bar{N}})=\prod_{\nu=0}^{{\bar{N}}-1}&\Big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta t_{\nu}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2\Delta t_{\nu}}[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}\Big)\Big\}\\
&\cdot(1+O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/4})).\end{split}

(3.12)

In particular, the factors
\exp(\frac{a}{2}t_{\bar{N}})
arising from (
3.10
) and (
3.11
) cancel.

We record our result (
3.12
) as a lemma.

Lemma 2

(Lemma on the Probability Distribution)
.

We condition on
a_{\emph{TRUE}}=a
,
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
.

Then, for constants
c,C
determined by
q_{0}
,
a_{\emph{MAX}}
,
C_{\emph{TAME}},
and an upper bound for
T
, the following holds.

Suppose
\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}}=\max_{\nu}(t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu})<c
. Fix
{\bar{N}}\leq N
. Let
\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\bar{N}})
be the joint probability density for
(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{\bar{N}}))
.

Let
Q>C
, and suppose
(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\bar{N}})
satisfies

\displaystyle\max_{\nu}(|\bar{q}_{\nu}|+|\bar{u}_{\nu}|)\leq Q\;\text{and}

\displaystyle\Big|\sum_{\nu}(\bar{q}_{\nu+1}-\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}-t_{\bar{N}}\Big|\leq Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}})^{1/4},

where
\bar{u}_{\nu}
is the control exercised by the strategy
\sigma
at time
t_{\nu}
when

(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))=(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\nu})\;\text{and}\;\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}.

Then

\displaystyle\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\bar{N}})=\prod_{\nu=0}^{{\bar{N}}-1}

\displaystyle\Big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta t_{\nu}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{1}{2\Delta t_{\nu}}[\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}-(a\bar{q}_{\nu}+\bar{u}_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}\Big)\Big\}

\displaystyle\cdot(1+O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}})^{1/4})).

Here,
\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}=\bar{q}_{\nu+1}-\bar{q}_{\nu}
(with
\bar{q}_{0}=q_{0}
),
\Delta t_{\nu}=t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu}
, and
O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}})^{1/4})
denotes a quantity whose absolute value is at most
CQ^{2}(\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}})^{1/4}
.

4
Analytic Continuation

In this section we prepare to make an analytic continuation of the function mapping
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
to the expected cost incurred by a strategy
\sigma
assuming that
a_{\text{MAX}}=a
. We set up notation.

We fix a tame strategy
\sigma=(\sigma_{\nu})_{0\leq\nu<N}
and coin flips
\vec{\eta}
; we write
\bar{u}_{\nu}
to denote the control exercised by the strategy
\sigma
at time
t_{\nu}
assuming that
q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu})=\bar{q}_{\mu}
for
1\leq\mu\leq\nu
and
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
(i.e.,
\bar{u}_{\nu}=u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})=\sigma_{\nu}(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\nu},\vec{\eta})
).

We define functions

\psi_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q},\bar{q},\bar{u},a):=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta t_{\nu}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{[\Delta\bar{q}-(a\bar{q}+\bar{u})\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}}{2\Delta t_{\nu}}\Big)

(4.1)

and

\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)=\prod_{\nu=0}^{N-1}\psi_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu},\bar{q}_{\nu},\bar{u}_{\nu}(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\nu}),a).

(4.2)

In (
4.1
),
\Delta\bar{q}
,
\bar{q}
,
\bar{u}
are real variables, while
a\in\mathbb{C}
. In (
4.2
),
\Delta\bar{q}_{\nu}:=\bar{q}_{\nu+1}-\bar{q}_{\nu}
, with
\bar{q}_{0}:=q_{0}
. Again, in (
4.2
),
a\in\mathbb{C}
.

We introduce a set

\begin{split}E=\{(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:&\max_{\nu}|\bar{q}_{\nu}|\leq(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{-1/16}\\
&\text{and}\;|\sum_{\nu}(\bar{q}_{\nu+1}-\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}-T|\leq(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/8}\}.\end{split}

(4.3)

We denote by

\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)\qquad(a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}])

the probability density for
(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{N}))
assuming that
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
and
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
.

According to Lemma
1
and Lemma
2
, we have

\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)=\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)\cdot(1+\text{ERR}(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a))

(4.4)

for
(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})\in E
and
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, with

|\text{ERR}(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)|\leq(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/8};

(4.5)

and

\text{E}_{a,\vec{\eta}}[\mathbbm{1}_{(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{N}))\notin E}]\leq C\exp(-c(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{-1/8}).

(4.6)

Since
|u^{\sigma}|\leq C[|q^{\sigma}|+1]
for a tame rule
\sigma
, we have, for
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
,

\sum_{\nu}\{(u^{\sigma}_{\nu})^{2}+(q^{\sigma}_{\nu})^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\leq C\max_{\nu}|q_{\nu}|^{2}+C.

(4.7)

By Lemma
1
, we have

\text{E}_{a,\vec{\eta}}[\{\max_{\nu}|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}^{2}]\leq C.

(4.8)

We study the function

[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]\ni a\mapsto\text{E}_{{a,\vec{\eta}}}\Big[\sum_{\nu=0}^{N-1}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big];

we denote this function by
\text{ECOST}(a)
.

The above definitions and estimates yield:

\begin{split}\text{ECOST}(a)=&\text{E}_{a,\vec{\eta}}\Big[\sum_{\nu=0}^{N-1}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{N}))\in E}\Big]\\
&+\text{ERROR 1}(a),\end{split}

(4.9)

with

\begin{split}|\text{ERROR 1}(a)|\leq&\text{E}_{a,\vec{\eta}}[C\max_{\nu}\{|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))\notin E}]\\
\leq&\Big(\text{E}_{a,\vec{\eta}}[C\max\{|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}^{2}])^{1/2}\\
&\cdot\Big(\text{Prob}_{a,\vec{\eta}}((q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{N}))\notin E)\Big)^{1/2}\\
\leq&C^{\prime}\exp(-c^{\prime}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{-1/8}).\end{split}

(4.10)

Moreover,

\displaystyle\text{E}_{a,\vec{\eta}}\Big[\sum_{\nu}

\displaystyle\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))\in E}\Big]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})\in E}\Big(\sum_{\nu}\{\bar{q}_{\nu}^{2}+\bar{u}_{\nu}^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)\ d\bar{q}_{1}\cdots d\bar{q}_{N}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\int_{(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})\in E}\Big\{\Big(\sum_{\nu}\{\bar{q}_{\nu}^{2}+\bar{u}_{\nu}^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)

\displaystyle\quad\cdot(1+\text{ERR}(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a))\ d\bar{q}_{1}\cdots d\bar{q}_{N}\Big\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(1+\text{ERROR 2}(a))\cdot\int_{(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})\in E}\Big\{\Big(\sum_{\nu}\{\bar{q}_{\nu}^{2}+\bar{u}_{\nu}^{2}\}(\Delta t_{\nu})\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\;\cdot\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)\ d\bar{q}_{1}\cdots d\bar{q}_{N}\Big\},

with

|\text{ERROR 2}(a)|\leq C(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/8},

(4.11)

thanks to (
4.4
), (
4.5
). Together with (
4.9
), (
4.10
), this yields

\displaystyle\text{ECOST}(a)=

\displaystyle\text{ERROR 1}(a)

\displaystyle+(1+\text{ERROR 2}(a))\cdot\int_{E}\Big\{\Big(\sum_{\nu}\{\bar{q}_{\nu}^{2}+\bar{u}_{\nu}^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\;\;\;\cdot\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)\Big\}\ d\bar{q}_{1}\cdots d\bar{q}_{N}

with
\text{ERROR 1}(a)
,
\text{ERROR 2}(a)
controlled by (
4.10
), (
4.11
). Since also

0\leq\text{ECOST}(a)\leq C

by Lemma
1
, it follows that

\begin{split}\text{ECOST}(a)=&\int_{(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})\in E}\Big\{\Big(\sum_{\nu}\{\bar{q}_{\nu}^{2}+\bar{u}_{\nu}^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\\
&\quad\cdot\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)\Big\}\;d\bar{q}_{1}\cdots d\bar{q}_{N}+\text{ERROR 3}(a),\end{split}

(4.12)

with

|\text{ERROR 3}(a)|\leq C(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/8}.

(4.13)

Equation (
4.12
) and estimate (
4.13
) hold for
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
. We make an analytic continuation of the integral in (
4.12
) from
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
to
a=a_{R}+ia_{I}
in the rectangle

\mathcal{R}=\{a_{R}+ia_{I}:a_{R}\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}],a_{I}\in[-\delta,\delta]\}

for a small
\delta>0
to be picked below.

We write
I(a)
to denote the integral in (
4.12
). Thus,

I(a)=\int_{E}\Big(\sum_{\nu}\{\bar{q}_{\nu}^{2}+\bar{u}_{\nu}^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)\ d\bar{q}_{1}\cdots d\bar{q}_{N}

(4.14)

for
a=a_{R}+ia_{I}\in\mathcal{R}
, and

|\text{ECOST}(a)-I(a)|\leq C(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/8}\;\text{for}\;a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}].

(4.15)

A glance at (
4.1
), (
4.2
) shows that the integrand in (
4.14
) has the form

B(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})\exp(a^{2}G_{2}(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})+aG_{1}(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})+G_{0}(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})),

where
B,G_{0},G_{1},G_{2}
are bounded measurable functions of
(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})
on
E
. Moreover, the region of integration,
E
, is bounded; see (
4.3
). Therefore,
I(a)
is an analytic function on
\mathcal{R}
.

Next, we estimate

\int_{E}\Big(\sum_{\nu}\{\bar{q}_{\nu}^{2}+\bar{u}_{\nu}^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)|\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a_{R}+ia_{I})|\;d\bar{q}_{1}\cdots d\bar{q}_{N}

(4.16)

for
a_{R}+ia_{I}\in\mathcal{R}
. From (
4.1
) we have

|\psi_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q},\bar{q},\bar{u},a_{R}+ia_{I})|=\exp(\frac{a_{I}^{2}}{2}\bar{q}^{2}\Delta t_{\nu})\psi_{\nu}(\Delta\bar{q},\bar{q},\bar{u},a_{R}),

hence (
4.2
) implies that

|\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a_{R}+ia_{I})|=\exp\Big(\sum_{\nu}\frac{a_{I}^{2}}{2}\bar{q}_{\nu}^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a_{R}).

Moreover, for
(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})\in E
and
a_{R}\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
, (
4.4
) and (
4.5
) yield

\Psi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a_{R})\leq 2\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a_{R}).

Consequently, the integrand in (
4.16
) is at most

2\Big(\sum_{\nu}\{\bar{q}_{\nu}^{2}+\bar{u}_{\nu}^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\exp\Big(\frac{a_{I}^{2}}{2}\sum_{\nu}\bar{q}_{\nu}^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a_{R})

for
a_{R}\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Since
\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a_{R})
is the probability density for
(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{N}))
assuming
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a_{R}
and
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
, it follows that the integral (
4.16
) is at most

2\text{E}_{a_{R},\vec{\eta}}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{N}))\in E}\cdot\Big(\sum_{\nu}\{q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\\
\cdot\exp\Big(\frac{a_{I}^{2}}{2}\sum_{\nu}(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\Big]\;\text{for}\;a\in\mathcal{R}.

Recall that
|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq C[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1]
and that
|a_{I}|\leq\delta
for
a=a_{R}+ia_{I}\in\mathcal{R}
.

Consequently, for
a_{R}+ia_{I}\in\mathcal{R}
, we have

\Big(\sum_{\nu}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\exp\Big(\frac{a_{I}^{2}}{2}\sum_{\nu}(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)\\
\leq C_{\delta}\cdot\exp\Big(C\delta^{2}\max_{\nu}|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\Big).

So the integral (
4.16
) is at most

C_{\delta}\text{E}_{a_{R},\vec{\eta}}\Big[\exp\Big(C\delta^{2}\max_{\nu}|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\Big)\Big]\;\text{for}\;a\in\mathcal{R}.

(4.17)

On the other hand, Lemma
1
gives

\text{E}_{a_{R},\vec{\eta}}[\exp(c\max_{\nu}|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2})]\leq C

(4.18)

for
a_{R}\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
and
c>0
small enough.

We now fix
\delta=\hat{c}
small enough that
C\delta^{2}<c
with
C,c
as in (
4.17
), (
4.18
). We conclude that the integral (
4.16
) is less than a large constant
C
, independent of
a\in\mathcal{R}
.

So we have shown that
I(a)
is analytic and bounded for

a\in(-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}})\times(-\delta,\delta).

Recalling that we have taken
\delta=\hat{c}
and that (
4.15
) holds, we obtain the following result.

Lemma 3

(Analytic Continuation Lemma)
.

Let
\sigma
be a tame strategy, and let
\vec{\eta}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
.

Then there exists an analytic function
I_{\vec{\eta}}(a)
on the rectangle

\mathcal{R}=\{a_{R}+ia_{I}:a_{R}\in(-a_{\emph{MAX}},a_{\emph{MAX}}),a_{I}\in(-\hat{c},\hat{c})\}

such that

|I_{\vec{\eta}}(a)|\leq C\;\text{on}\;\mathcal{R}

and

\Big|\emph{E}_{a,\vec{\eta}}\Big[\sum_{\nu=0}^{N-1}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]-I_{\vec{\eta}}(a)\Big|\leq C(\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}})^{1/8}

for
a\in(-a_{\emph{MAX}},+a_{\emph{MAX}})
. Explicitly,

\displaystyle I_{\vec{\eta}}(a)=

\displaystyle\int_{E}\Big(\sum_{\nu=0}^{N-1}\{q_{\nu}^{2}+u_{\nu}^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)

\displaystyle\cdot\prod_{\nu=0}^{N-1}\Big\{\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta t_{\nu}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{[q_{\nu+1}-q_{\nu}-(aq_{\nu}+u_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}]^{2}}{2\Delta t_{\nu}}\Big)\Big\}\ dq_{1}\cdots dq_{N}

where

\begin{split}E=\{(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})\in\mathbb{R}^{N}:&\max_{\nu}|\bar{q}_{\nu}|\leq(\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}})^{-1/16}\\
&\emph{and}\;|\sum_{\nu}(\bar{q}_{\nu+1}-\bar{q}_{\nu})^{2}-T|\leq(\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}})^{1/8}\}.\end{split}

(4.19)

and
u_{\nu}
denotes the value assigned by
\sigma
to the control at time
t_{\nu}
assuming that
q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu})=q_{\mu}
for
0\leq\mu\leq\nu
and
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
.

5
Moments of Increments

We retain the assumptions and notation of Section
2
. Recall that
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
is the sigma algebra of events determined by
q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu})
(
0\leq\mu\leq\nu)
, and that

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\nu}=t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu},

\displaystyle\Delta q_{\nu}=q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}=\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}=\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}).

We suppose that
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
and
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
.

Recall also that

\displaystyle\Delta q_{\nu}=(aq_{\nu}+u_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}+\Delta W_{\nu},

(5.1)

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}=q_{\nu}(\Delta q_{\nu}-u_{\nu}\Delta t_{\nu})=aq_{\nu}^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}+q_{\nu}u_{\nu}(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}-\Delta t_{\nu})+q_{\nu}\Delta W_{\nu},

(5.2)

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}=q_{\nu}^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}.

(5.3)

We condition on
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
; thus,
q_{\nu}
and
u_{\nu}
are deterministic, while
\Delta W_{\nu}
is normal, with mean 0 and variance
\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}
. We suppose that

|q_{\nu}|,|u_{\nu}|\leq Q,\;\text{with}\;Q\geq C\;\text{given}.

(5.4)

Then

\displaystyle\Delta q_{\nu}=O(Q)\Delta t_{\nu}+\Delta W_{\nu},

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}=O(Q^{2})\Delta t_{\nu}+q_{\nu}\Delta W_{\nu},

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}=O(Q^{2})\Delta t_{\nu},

so that

\displaystyle(\Delta q_{\nu})^{2}=O(Q^{2})(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}+2O(Q)\Delta t_{\nu}\Delta W_{\nu}+(\Delta W_{\nu})^{2},

(5.5)

\displaystyle(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu})(\Delta q_{\nu})=O(Q^{3})(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}+O(Q^{2})\Delta t_{\nu}\Delta W_{\nu}+q_{\nu}(\Delta W_{\nu})^{2},

(5.6)

\displaystyle(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu})(\Delta q_{\nu})=O(Q^{3})(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}+O(Q^{2})\Delta t_{\nu}\Delta W_{\nu},

(5.7)

\displaystyle(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu})^{2}=O(Q^{4})(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}+O(Q^{3})(\Delta t_{\nu})(\Delta W_{\nu})+q_{\nu}^{2}(\Delta W_{\nu})^{2},

(5.8)

\displaystyle(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu})(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu})=O(Q^{4})(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}+O(Q^{3})(\Delta t_{\nu})(\Delta W_{\nu})

(5.9)

\displaystyle(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu})^{2}=O(Q^{4})(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}.

(5.10)

Moreover, all the quantities
O(Q^{\text{power}})
above are deterministic once we condition on
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
.

Therefore, (
5.1
)–(
5.3
) and (
5.5
)–(
5.10
) yield the following.

\displaystyle\text{E}[\Delta q_{\nu}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=(aq_{\nu}+u_{\nu})\Delta t_{\nu}+O(Q(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}),

(5.11)

\displaystyle\text{E}[\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=aq_{\nu}^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})+O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}),

(5.12)

\displaystyle\text{E}[\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=q_{\nu}^{2}\Delta t_{\nu},

(5.13)

\displaystyle\text{E}[(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=q_{\nu}^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}+O(Q^{4}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}),

(5.14)

\displaystyle\text{E}[(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu})(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu})|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=O(Q^{4}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}),,

(5.15)

\displaystyle\text{E}[(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=O(Q^{4}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}),

(5.16)

\displaystyle\text{E}[(\Delta q_{\nu})^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=\Delta t_{\nu}+O(Q^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}),

(5.17)

\displaystyle\text{E}[(\Delta q_{\nu})(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu})|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=q_{\nu}\Delta t_{\nu}+O(Q^{3}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}),

(5.18)

\displaystyle\text{E}[(\Delta q_{\nu})(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu})|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=O(Q^{3}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}).

(5.19)

(Here we have used the fact that
\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}
and
\Delta\tilde{t}_{\nu}
are
\Delta t_{\nu}+O((\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}).
)

We define an event

\text{TAME}_{\nu}=\{|\Delta q_{\nu}|\leq 2(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5},|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}|\leq 2(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5},\\
|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}|\leq 2(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5}\}.

(5.20)

From Lemma
1
we obtain the estimate

\text{E}[(\Delta q_{\nu})^{\alpha_{0}}(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu})^{\alpha_{1}}(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu})^{\alpha_{2}}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\text{NOT TAME}_{\nu}}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=O((\Delta t_{\nu})^{100})

for integers
\alpha_{0},\alpha_{1},\alpha_{2}\geq 0
with
\alpha_{0}+\alpha_{1}+\alpha_{2}\leq 2
. Also from Lemma
1
, we recall that

\text{Prob}[\text{NOT TAME}_{\nu}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq C\cdot(\Delta t_{\nu})^{1000}.

Consequently, (
5.11
)–(
5.19
) imply the conclusions of the following lemma.

Lemma 4

(Lemma on Moments of Increments)
.

We fix
a_{\emph{TRUE}}=a
and
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
.

We suppose that

Q\geq C\text{ and }(\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}})<Q^{-1000}.

Define the event

\emph{TAME}(\nu)=\{|\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|\leq 2(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5},|\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|\leq 2(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5},|\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|\leq 2(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2/5}\}.

Let
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
be the sigma algebra of events determined by
q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu})
(
0\leq\mu\leq\nu)
.

Fix
\nu
, and suppose that

|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|,|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq Q.

Then the following hold.

\displaystyle\emph{E}[(\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu})\mathbbm{1}_{\emph{TAME}(\nu)}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=(aq^{\sigma}_{\nu}+u^{\sigma}_{\nu})(\Delta t_{\nu})+\emph{ERR}\;1,

\displaystyle\emph{E}[(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma})\mathbbm{1}_{\emph{TAME}(\nu)}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=a(q^{\sigma}_{\nu})^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})+\emph{ERR}\;2,

\displaystyle\emph{E}[(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma})\mathbbm{1}_{\emph{TAME}(\nu)}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=(q^{\sigma}_{\nu})^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})+\emph{ERR}\;3

\displaystyle\emph{E}[(\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu})^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\emph{TAME}(\nu)}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=(\Delta t_{\nu})+\emph{ERR}\;4,

\displaystyle\emph{E}[(\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu})(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma})\mathbbm{1}_{\emph{TAME}(\nu)}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=q^{\sigma}_{\nu}(\Delta t_{\nu})+\emph{ERR}\;5,

\displaystyle\emph{E}[(\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu})(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma})\mathbbm{1}_{\emph{TAME}(\nu)}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=\emph{ERR}\;6,

\displaystyle\emph{E}[(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma})^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\emph{TAME}(\nu)}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=(q^{\sigma}_{\nu})^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})+\emph{ERR}\;7,

\displaystyle\emph{E}[(\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma})(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma})\mathbbm{1}_{\emph{TAME}(\nu)}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=\emph{ERR}\;8,

\displaystyle\emph{E}[(\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma})^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\emph{TAME}(\nu)}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=\emph{ERR}\;9,

where

|\emph{ERR}\;1|,\dots,|\emph{ERR}\;9|\leq C^{\prime}Q^{4}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}.

Also, under the above assumptions, we have

\emph{Prob}[\emph{NOTTAME}(\nu)|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq(\Delta t_{\nu})^{20}.

Here, of course,

\displaystyle q^{\sigma}_{\nu}=q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),

\displaystyle u^{\sigma}_{\nu}=u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),

\displaystyle\Delta t_{\nu}=t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu},

\displaystyle\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}=q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}=\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}=\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}).

The constants
C,C^{\prime}
are determined by
q_{0},a_{\emph{MAX}},C_{\emph{TAME}}
and an upper bound for
T
.

6
Stability Under Change of Assumption

Let
f(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N})
be a nonnegative function on
\mathbb{R}^{N}
. For
\vec{\eta}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
and
a_{1},a_{2}\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
, we compare

\text{E}_{a_{1},\vec{\eta}}[f(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{N}))]

with

\text{E}_{a_{2},\vec{\eta}}[f(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{N}))]

for a tame strategy
\sigma
.

To do so, let
\Phi(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{N},a)
denote the probability density of

(q^{\sigma}(t_{1}),\dots,q^{\sigma}(t_{N}))

assuming that
