&\geq\text{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq t_{\mu}<\tau}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big].\end{split}

(5.43)

Substituting (
5.43
), (
5.42
), (
5.38
) into (
5.36
), we find that

\displaystyle\text{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}\{(q^{\sigma}

\displaystyle(t_{\mu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]+CQ^{2m_{0}}\exp(-cQ)

\displaystyle\geq

\displaystyle S(q_{0},0,0,0)+\text{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Big)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\textsc{non-disaster}}\Big]

\displaystyle-Q^{2m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/20}T.

Together with (
5.40
), this in turn yields:

\begin{split}\text{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}&(t_{\mu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]\\
\geq&S(q_{0},0,0,0)+\text{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Big)\Big]\\
&-CQ^{2m_{0}}\exp(-cQ)-Q^{2m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/20}T.\end{split}

(5.44)

Similarly, suppose
\sigma=\tilde{\sigma}
. Then, substituting (
5.41
) and (
5.38
) into (
5.37
), we find that

\text{E}\Big[\Big(\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big)\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\textsc{non-disaster}}\Big]\\
\leq S(q_{0},0,0,0)+CQ^{2m_{0}}\exp(-cQ)+Q^{2m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/20}T.

Together with (
5.39
), this implies that

\begin{split}\text{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}&\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]\\
&\leq S(q_{0},0,0,0)+CQ^{2m_{0}}\exp(-cQ)+Q^{2m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/20}T.\end{split}

(5.45)

We have proven (
5.44
) for
Q\geq C
and
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}\leq Q^{-2000m_{0}}
; see (
5.1
). Similarly, (
5.45
) holds for
Q\geq C
,
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}\leq Q^{-2000m_{0}}
,
\sigma=\tilde{\sigma}
.

Now suppose
\varepsilon>0
is given. We pick
Q\geq C
so large that

CQ^{2m_{0}}\exp(-cQ)<\varepsilon/2

in (
5.44
), (
5.45
), and also so large that

Q^{2m_{0}}(Q^{-2000m_{0}})^{1/20}T<\varepsilon/2.

Then, if
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}<Q^{-2000m_{0}}
, we obtain from (
5.44
) and (
5.45
) the estimates

\text{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]\\
\geq S(q_{0},0,0,0)+\text{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]-\varepsilon,

and if
\sigma=\tilde{\sigma}
then

\text{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]\leq S(q_{0},0,0,0)+\varepsilon.

Thus, we have proven the following result,
modulo
our PDE Assumption.

Lemma 14

(First Bayesian Main Lemma)
.

Let
\tilde{\sigma}
be the
allegedly optimal strategy
for the partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=T
, and let
\varepsilon>0
be given.

Let
\sigma
be another tame deterministic strategy for the same partition. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies

|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq C_{\emph{TAME}}^{\sigma}[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1].

Suppose that

\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}}=\max_{\nu}(t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu})

is less than a small enough
\delta>0
, determined by
\varepsilon
,
C_{\emph{TAME}}^{\sigma}
, and the
boilerplate constants
. Then

\emph{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\\
\geq S(q_{0},0,0,0)+\emph{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]-\varepsilon

and

\emph{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{(q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\leq S(q_{0},0,0,0)+\varepsilon,

where

\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})=u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u_{\emph{opt}}(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),t_{\nu},\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})).

Corollary 2
.

Let
\sigma
,
\tilde{\sigma}
be as above. If

\emph{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\\
\leq\emph{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{(q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]+\varepsilon,

then

\emph{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\leq 3\varepsilon.

6
Stability of the Allegedly Optimal Strategy

We begin by setting up the notation for this section.

•

\tilde{\sigma}
denotes the
allegedly optimal strategy
.

•

\sigma
denotes some other tame deterministic strategy based on the same partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=T
as
\tilde{\sigma}
.

•

q^{\sigma}_{\nu}
denotes
q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
, and
\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}
denotes
q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
.

•

q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}
denotes
q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})
, and
\Delta q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}
denotes
q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu+1})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})
.

•

\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}
denotes
\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
, and
\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}
denotes
\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
.

•

\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
denotes
\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})
, and
\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
denotes
\zeta_{1}^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu+1})-\zeta_{1}^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})
.

•

\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}
denotes
\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
, and
\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}
denotes
\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu+1})-\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
.

•

\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
denotes
\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})
, and
\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
denotes
\zeta_{2}^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu+1})-\zeta_{2}^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})
.

•

u^{\sigma}_{\nu}
denotes
u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
, and
u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}
denotes
u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})
.

We recall that

|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma}[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1],\qquad|u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|\leq C_{\text{TAME}}^{\text{opt}}[|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|+1].

In this section,
c
,
C
,
C^{\prime}
, etc. denote constants determined by
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma}
and the
boilerplate constants
(one of which is
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\text{opt}}
). As usual, these symbols may denote different constants in different occurrences.

We fix
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
, and condition on
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
. We write
\text{Prob}_{a}[\cdots]
and
\text{E}_{a}[\cdots]
to denote the corresponding probability and expectation, respectively.

We write
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
to denote the sigma algebra of events determined by the Brownian motion
(W(t))_{t\in[0,t_{\nu}]}
(and by
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
).

Recall that

\displaystyle\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}=(aq^{\sigma}_{\nu}+u^{\sigma}_{\nu})(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})+\Delta W_{\nu}

(6.1)

\displaystyle\Delta q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}=(aq^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}+u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu})(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})+\Delta W_{\nu},

(6.2)

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}=q^{\sigma}_{\nu}(\Delta q^{\sigma}_{\nu}-u^{\sigma}_{\nu}\Delta t_{\nu})

(6.3)

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}=q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}(\Delta q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}\Delta t_{\nu})

(6.4)

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}=(q^{\sigma}_{\nu})^{2}\Delta t_{\nu},

(6.5)

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}=(q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu})^{2}\Delta t_{\nu},

(6.6)

and that

q^{\sigma}_{0}=q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{0}=q_{0},\qquad\zeta_{1,0}^{\sigma}=\zeta_{1,0}^{\tilde{\sigma}}=\zeta_{2,0}^{\sigma}=\zeta_{2,0}^{\tilde{\sigma}}=0.

(6.7)

In (
6.1
) and (
6.2
),
\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}=\Delta t_{\nu}+O((\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}),
and
\Delta W_{\nu}
is a normal random variable with mean 0 and variance
O(\Delta t_{\nu})
, independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
.

The quantities
q^{\sigma}_{\nu}
,
q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}
,
u^{\sigma}_{\nu}
,
u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}
,
\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}
,
\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
,
\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}
,
\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}
are deterministic once we condition on
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}^{\sigma}
.

As in Section
5
, we define

\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})=u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u_{\text{opt}}(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),t_{\nu},\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})).

(6.8)

Our goal is to show that if

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\;\text{is small},

(6.9)

then also

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\;\text{is small}.

(6.10)

Here, (
6.9
) asserts that
\sigma
does something close to what
\tilde{\sigma}
would do in the circumstances encountered by
\sigma
. On the other hand, (
6.10
) asserts that
\sigma
and
\tilde{\sigma}
produce nearly equal outcomes. To show that (
6.9
) implies (
6.10
), we introduce the vector

\mathcal{X}_{\nu}=\begin{pmatrix}q^{\sigma}_{\nu}-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}\\
\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}-\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}\\
\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}-\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}\end{pmatrix}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}\\
\mathcal{X}_{\nu,2}\\
\mathcal{X}_{\nu,3}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{3}.

(6.11)

Thanks to (
6.7
), we have

\mathcal{X}_{0}=0.

(6.12)

For a large enough
C
, we introduce a positive number
Q
satisfying

Q\geq C.

(6.13)

Under the assumption

|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|,|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|,|\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|,|\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}|,|\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|,|\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}|\leq Q,

(6.14)

we will estimate

\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu}\equiv\begin{pmatrix}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}\\
\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu,2}\\
\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu,3}\end{pmatrix}\equiv\mathcal{X}_{\nu+1}-\mathcal{X}_{\nu}.

Until further notice we fix
\nu
and assume (
6.14
).

We write
G_{1},G_{2},\dots
to denote random variables satisfying

\displaystyle G_{i}\;\text{is deterministic once we condition on }\mathcal{F}_{\nu}\text{ and}

(6.15)

\displaystyle|G_{i}|\leq CQ^{m_{0}},\;\text{with }m_{0}\;\text{as in our PDE Assumption (see \eqref{eq: pde 5}.}

(6.16)

To estimate
\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu}
, we first apply our PDE Assumption (see (
3.5
)) to show that

\displaystyle u_{\text{opt}}(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),t_{\nu},

\displaystyle\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle u_{\text{opt}}(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),t_{\nu},\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))-u_{\text{opt}}(q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}),t_{\nu},\zeta_{1}^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}),\zeta_{2}^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle G_{1}[q^{\sigma}_{\nu}-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}]+G_{2}[\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}-\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}]+G_{3}[\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}-\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}]

with
G_{1}
,
G_{2}
,
G_{3}
as in (
6.15
), (
6.16
).

Consequently, (
6.8
) implies that

u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})=\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})+G_{1}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}+G_{2}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,2}+G_{3}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,3}.

(6.17)

We subtract (
6.2
) from (
6.1
) and apply (
6.17
). Thus,

\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}=a\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})+\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})\\
+G_{1}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})+G_{2}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,2}(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})+G_{3}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,3}(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}),

which implies that

\begin{split}\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}=G_{4}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}(\Delta t_{\nu})+G_{5}&\mathcal{X}_{\nu,2}(\Delta t_{\nu})\\
&+G_{6}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,3}(\Delta t_{\nu})+\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})\cdot(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})\end{split}

(6.18)

with
G_{4}
,
G_{5}
,
G_{6}
satisfying (
6.15
), (
6.16
). Next, we deduce from (
6.1
), (
6.3
) that

\displaystyle\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}

\displaystyle=q^{\sigma}_{\nu}\cdot\big([(aq^{\sigma}_{\nu}+u^{\sigma}_{\nu})(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})+\Delta W_{\nu}]-u^{\sigma}_{\nu}\Delta t_{\nu}\big)

\displaystyle=a(q^{\sigma}_{\nu})^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})+q^{\sigma}_{\nu}\Delta W_{\nu}+q^{\sigma}_{\nu}\cdot u^{\sigma}_{\nu}\cdot(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}-\Delta t_{\nu}),

and similarly

\Delta\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}=a(q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu})^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*})+q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}\Delta W_{\nu}+q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}\cdot u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}\cdot(\Delta t_{\nu}^{*}-\Delta t_{\nu}).

Subtracting, and recalling our assumptions (
6.14
), we find that

\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu,2}=G_{7}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}(\Delta t_{\nu})+\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}\Delta W_{\nu}+G_{8}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2},

(6.19)

with
G_{7}
,
G_{8}
as in (
6.15
), (
6.16
). (Here, we use also the estimate

|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq C[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1]

as well as the corresponding estimate for
u^{\tilde{\sigma}}
,
q^{\tilde{\sigma}}
.)

Again recalling (
6.14
), we see from (
6.5
), (
6.6
) that

\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu,3}=G_{9}\mathcal{X}_{\nu,1}(\Delta t_{\nu}),

(6.20)

with
G_{9}
as in (
6.15
), (
6.16
).

Equations (
6.18
), (
6.19
), (
6.20
) tell us that

\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu}=G\mathcal{X}_{\nu}(\Delta t_{\nu})+H\mathcal{X}_{\nu}(\Delta W_{\nu})+F_{\nu},

(6.21)

where:

•

The entries of the matrix
G
satisfy (
6.15
), (
6.16
).

•

H
is the constant matrix

\begin{pmatrix}0&0&0\\
1&0&0\\
0&0&0\end{pmatrix}.

•

The vector
F_{\nu}
satisfies

|F_{\nu}|\leq C|\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\cdot(\Delta t_{\nu})+CQ^{m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}.

We now estimate

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}[|\mathcal{X}_{\nu+1}|^{2}-|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]=&2\text{E}_{a}[(\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu})\cdot\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]+\text{E}_{a}[|\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\\
\equiv&2\cdot\textsc{Term}1+\textsc{Term}2.\end{split}

(6.22)

Recall that
G
,
\mathcal{X}_{\nu}
are deterministic once we condition on
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
, while
\Delta W_{\nu}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
, with mean 0 and variance
\leq C(\Delta t_{\nu})
. Hence, from (
6.21
) we have the following estimates.

\displaystyle\textsc{Term}1=

\displaystyle\text{E}_{a}[\mathcal{X}_{\nu}\cdot(\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu})|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle CQ^{m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\nu})|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}+C|\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\cdot|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|(\Delta t_{\nu})

\displaystyle+C|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|Q^{m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}.

\displaystyle\textsc{Term}2=

\displaystyle\text{E}_{a}[|\Delta\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle C|G\mathcal{X}_{\nu}(\Delta t_{\nu})|^{2}+C|H\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}\text{E}_{a}[|\Delta W_{\nu}|^{2}]

\displaystyle+C(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}+CQ^{2m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{4}.

Our assumption (
6.14
) implies that
|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|\leq CQ
, hence the above estimates imply that

\begin{split}\textsc{Term}1\leq&CQ^{m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\nu})|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}+C(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})\\
&+CQ^{m_{0}+1}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}\end{split}

(6.23)

and

\begin{split}\textsc{Term}2\leq&CQ^{2m_{0}+2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}+C|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})\\
&+C(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}+CQ^{2m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{4}.\end{split}

(6.24)

Putting (
6.23
) and (
6.24
) into (
6.22
), we learn that

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}[|\mathcal{X}_{\nu+1}|^{2}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq&(1+CQ^{m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\nu}))|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}+C(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\\
&+CQ^{2m_{0}+2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}.\end{split}

(6.25)

We have proven (
6.25
) under the assumption (
6.14
). We now drop assumption (
6.14
), and let
\mathcal{E}_{\nu}
denote the event

\left[\begin{array}[]{l}|q^{\sigma}_{\mu}|,|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\mu}|,|\zeta_{1,\mu}^{\sigma}|,|\zeta_{1,\mu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}|,|\zeta_{2,\mu}^{\sigma}|,|\zeta_{2,\mu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}|\leq Q\\
\text{for all}\;\mu\leq\nu.\end{array}\right]

Note that
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\nu+1}}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\nu}}
, and that (
6.14
) holds whenever
\mathcal{E}_{\nu}
occurs. Moreover,
\mathcal{E}_{\nu}
is deterministic once we condition on
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
. Therefore, from (
6.25
) we deduce that

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}[|\mathcal{X}_{\nu+1}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\nu+1}}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\leq&\text{E}_{a}[|\mathcal{X}_{\nu+1}|^{2}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\nu}}|\mathcal{F}_{\nu}]\\
\leq&(1+CQ^{m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\nu}))|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\nu}}\\
&+C(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}+CQ^{2m_{0}+2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}.\end{split}

(6.26)

We now cease conditioning on
\mathcal{F}_{\nu}
, and condition merely on
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
. From (
6.26
) we learn that

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}[|\mathcal{X}_{\nu+1}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\nu+1}}]\leq&(1+CQ^{m_{0}}(\Delta t_{\nu}))\text{E}_{a}[|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\nu}}]\\
&+C\text{E}_{a}[(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}](\Delta t_{\nu})+CQ^{2m_{0}+2}(\Delta t_{\nu})^{2}.\end{split}

(6.27)

Recall that
\mathcal{X}_{0}=0
.

We impose the smallness assumption

(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\leq Q^{-(2m_{0}+2)}.

(6.28)

Then (
6.27
) implies that

\displaystyle\text{E}_{a}[|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\nu}}]\leq C\exp(CQ^{m_{0}}t_{\nu})\cdot\Big\{\text{E}_{a}

\displaystyle\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<\nu}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]

\displaystyle+C(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big\}

for
0\leq\nu\leq N
. Since
\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\leq\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{\nu}}
, it follows that

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}[|\mathcal{X}_{\nu}|^{2}\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}]\leq C\exp(CQ^{m_{0}})\Big\{\text{E}_{a}&\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]\\
&+(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big\}.\end{split}

(6.29)

In particular, since
q^{\sigma}_{\nu}-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}
is the first component of
\mathcal{X}_{\nu}
, we have

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}[|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}\cdot&\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}]\\
\leq&C\exp(CQ^{m_{0}})\Big\{\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big\}.\end{split}

(6.30)

Moreover, (
6.17
) and (
6.29
) together yield:

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}[|u^{\sigma}_{\nu}-&u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}]\\
\leq&CQ^{2m_{0}}\exp(CQ^{m_{0}})\Big\{\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big\}\\
&+C\text{E}_{a}[(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}].\end{split}

(6.31)

Summing (
6.30
) and (
6.31
) against
\Delta t_{\nu}
, we find that

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\big\{|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}&-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}_{\nu}-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}\big\}\Delta t_{\nu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}}\Big]\\
\leq&CQ^{2m_{0}}\exp(CQ^{m_{0}})\Big\{\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad+(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big\}.\end{split}

(6.32)

We now turn to the case in which
\mathcal{E}_{N}
doesn’t occur. Recall that
\mathcal{E}_{N}
fails precisely when, for some
\nu
, we have

\max\{|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|,|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|,|\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\sigma}|,|\zeta_{1,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}|,|\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\sigma}|,|\zeta_{2,\nu}^{\tilde{\sigma}}|\}>Q.

Thanks to Lemma
1
, applied to the strategies
\sigma
and
\tilde{\sigma}
, we have

\text{Prob}_{a}[\mathcal{E}_{N}\;\text{fails}]\leq C\exp(-cQ)

(6.33)

and also that

\text{E}_{a}[(\max_{\nu}\{|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|+|u^{\sigma}_{\nu}|+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|\})^{4}]\leq C,

hence

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\Big(\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\big\{|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}_{\nu}-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}\big\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big)^{2}\Big]\leq C.

(6.34)

From (
6.33
), (
6.34
), and Cauchy-Schwarz, we obtain the estimate

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\big\{|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}_{\nu}-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}\big\}\Delta t_{\nu}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\mathcal{E}_{N}\;\text{fails}}\Big]\leq C\exp(-cQ).

(6.35)

Finally, combining (
6.32
) and (
6.35
), we find that

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\big\{|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}&-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}_{\nu}+u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}\big\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\\
\leq&CQ^{2m_{0}}\exp(CQ^{m_{0}})\Big\{\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]\\
&\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad+(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\Big\}+C\exp(-cQ).\end{split}

(6.36)

We have proven (
6.36
) under the assumption (
6.28
).

Now let
\varepsilon>0
be given.

We take
Q
in (
6.36
) large enough so that
C\exp(-cQ)<\varepsilon/3
.

Having picked
Q
, we strengthen our smallness assumption (
6.28
) by demanding that

CQ^{2m_{0}}\exp(CQ^{m_{0}})\cdot(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{1/2}\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}.

If also

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\mu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\mu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\mu}\Big]\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{3}[CQ^{2m_{0}}\exp(CQ^{m_{0}})]^{-1},

then (
6.36
) implies that

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\big\{|q^{\sigma}_{\nu}-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}_{\nu}-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{\nu}|^{2}\big\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<\varepsilon.

Thus, we have proven the following result.

Lemma 15
.

[Stability Lemma]
Let
\varepsilon>0
, let
\tilde{\sigma}
be the
allegedly optimal strategy
for the partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=T
, and let
\sigma
be another tame deterministic strategy for that same partition. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies

|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq C_{\emph{TAME}}^{\sigma}[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1].

Fix
a\in[-a_{\emph{MAX}},+a_{\emph{MAX}}]
. Suppose that

\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}}=\max_{\nu}(t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu})<\delta

and

\emph{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u_{\emph{opt}}(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),t_{\nu},\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<\delta

for a small enough
\delta>0
determined by
\varepsilon
,
C_{\emph{TAME}}^{\sigma}
, and the
boilerplate constants
. Then

\emph{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\big\{|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\big\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<\varepsilon.

7
The Second Bayesian Main Lemma

Lemma 16

(The Second Bayesian Main Lemma)
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
, let
\tilde{\sigma}
be the
allegedly optimal strategy
for a partition of
[0,T]
, and let
\sigma
be another deterministic tame strategy for that same partition. Assume that
\sigma
satisfies

|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq C_{\emph{TAME}}^{\sigma}[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1].

Suppose that

\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}}<\delta

and that

\begin{split}\emph{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(&u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\\
&\leq\emph{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{(q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]+\delta,\end{split}

(7.1)

for a small enough
\delta>0
determined by
\varepsilon
,
C_{\emph{TAME}}^{\sigma}
, and the
boilerplate constants
.

Then, for every
a\in[-a_{\emph{MAX}},+a_{\emph{MAX}}]
, we have

\emph{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<\varepsilon.

Proof.

We first note that we can replace the
boilerplate constant

C_{\text{TAME}}^{\text{opt}}
by
\max\{C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma},C_{\text{TAME}}^{\text{opt}}\}
.

Observe that

\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}):=u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u_{\text{opt}}(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),t_{\nu},\zeta_{1}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}),\zeta_{2}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))

satisfies

|\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+C[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1],

hence

\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\leq C^{\prime}\max_{0\leq\nu<N}\{|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+1\}.

Lemma
1
therefore yields the estimate

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\Big\{\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big\}^{p}\Big]\leq C_{p}

(7.2)

for any
p\geq 1
and any
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
.

Now let
\varepsilon>0
be given. We pick a small enough
\varepsilon_{1}>0
depending on
\varepsilon
, then we pick a large enough
Q>1
depending on
\varepsilon_{1}
, next we pick a small enough
\varepsilon_{2}>0
depending on
Q
, and finally we pick a small enough
\delta>0
depending on
\varepsilon_{2}
. We then argue as follows.

Suppose that
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}<\delta
, and suppose (
7.1
) holds. Since
\delta>0
has been picked small enough, depending on
\varepsilon_{2}
, Corollary
2
tells us that

\text{E}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<3\varepsilon_{2}.

(7.3)

For any random variable
X
, the expected value
\text{E}[X]
is an average of
\text{E}_{a}[X]
over
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
with respect to our given prior probability distribution for
a_{\text{TRUE}}
. Therefore, (
7.3
) implies that for some
a_{1}\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
we have

\text{E}_{a_{1}}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<4\varepsilon_{2}.

Consequently, for any
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
, Lemma
5
and estimate (
7.2
) with
p=2
give

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\leq\exp(CQ^{2})\cdot 4\varepsilon_{2}+C\exp(-cQ^{2}).

(7.4)

Since
\varepsilon_{2}
has been picked small enough depending on
Q
, while
Q
has been picked large enough depending on
\varepsilon_{1}
, the right-hand side of (
7.4
) is less than
C^{\prime}\exp(-cQ^{2})<\varepsilon_{1}
. Thus, for any
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, we have

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}(\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<\varepsilon_{1}.

(7.5)

Since
\varepsilon_{1}
has been picked small enough depending on
\varepsilon
, estimate (
7.5
) and Lemma
15
imply that

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<\varepsilon

for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 3
.

Under the assumptions of Lemma
16
we have

\bigg|\emph{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\\
-\emph{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{(q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\bigg|\leq C\varepsilon^{1/2}

for each
a\in[-a_{\emph{MAX}},a_{\emph{MAX}}]
.

Proof.

The Corollary follows from Lemma
16
, together with Minkowski’s inequality and the estimate

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{(q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]\leq C,

which in turn follows from Lemma
1
.
∎

8
Allowing for Dependence on Coin Flips

Let
\tilde{\sigma}
be the
allegedly optimal strategy
associated to a partition of
[0,T]
, and let
\sigma
be a tame strategy associated to the same partition. In this section we allow
\sigma
to depend on the coin flips
\vec{\xi}
. For fixed
\vec{\eta}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, we write
\sigma_{\vec{\eta}}
for the strategy prescribed by
\sigma
in case
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
. We write
\text{Prob}_{B}[\dots]
and
\text{E}_{B}[\dots]
(“B” for “Bernoulli”) to denote probability and expectation with respect to the natural (product) probability measure on
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
, in which each
\xi_{\nu}
is equal to 0 with probability
1/2.

Our goal here is to extend Lemma
16
to the case of the
\vec{\xi}
-dependent strategy
\sigma
.

To so we denote

\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\sigma)=\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{(q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}+(u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu}))^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu},

and similarly for
\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\tilde{\sigma})
and
\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\sigma_{\vec{\eta}})
.

Because
\sigma
is tame, we have

|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma}\cdot[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1]

(8.1)

for a constant
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma}
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be given, and let
\delta>0
be less than a small enough positive number determined by
\varepsilon
,
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma}
, and the
boilerplate constants
.

Suppose that
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}<\delta
, and that

\text{E}[\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\sigma)]\leq\text{E}[\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\tilde{\sigma})]+\delta.

(8.2)

We want to show that

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<\varepsilon

for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. To see this, we first pick
\hat{\delta}
small enough, determined by
\varepsilon
,
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma}
, and the
boilerplate constants
; then we pick
\delta
small enough, determined by
\hat{\delta}
,
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma}
, and the
boilerplate constants
.

Lemma
14
, with
\hat{\delta}^{2}
in place of
\varepsilon
, shows that

\text{E}[\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\sigma_{\vec{\eta}})]\geq\text{E}[\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\tilde{\sigma})]-\hat{\delta}^{2}\;\text{for all}\;\vec{\eta}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}.

(8.3)

On the other hand, (
8.2
) shows that

\text{E}_{B}[\{\text{E}[\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\sigma_{\vec{\eta}})]-\text{E}[\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\tilde{\sigma})]+\hat{\delta}^{2}\}]\leq\delta+\hat{\delta}^{2}<2\hat{\delta}^{2},

(8.4)

since
\delta
is less than a small enough constant depending on
\hat{\delta}
. The quantity in curly brackets in (
8.4
) is nonnegative, thanks to (
8.3
). Therefore, if we set

\textsc{goodflips}=\{\vec{\eta}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\text{E}[\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\sigma_{\vec{\eta}})]\leq\text{E}[\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\tilde{\sigma})]+\hat{\delta}\}

(8.5)

and

\textsc{badflips}=\{\vec{\eta}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}:\text{E}[\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\sigma_{\vec{\eta}})]>\text{E}[\textsc{Cost}_{D}(\tilde{\sigma})]+\hat{\delta}\},

(8.6)

then

\text{Prob}_{B}[\textsc{badflips}]<10\hat{\delta}.

(8.7)

Moreover, since
\sigma_{\vec{\eta}}
and
\tilde{\sigma}
are tame for each
\vec{\eta}
, Lemma
1
implies that for each
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
we have

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\Big\{\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}[|q^{\sigma_{\vec{\eta}}}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma_{\vec{\eta}}}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}]\Delta t_{\nu}\Big\}^{2}\Big]\leq C,

where we allow constants
C
to depend on
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma}
in (
8.1
). Consequently,

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\Big\{\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}]\Delta t_{\nu}\Big\}^{2}\Big]\leq C.

Together with (
8.7
) and Cauchy-Schwarz, this implies that

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}\Big[\Big\{\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}]\Delta t_{\nu}\Big\}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\textsc{badflips}}(\vec{\xi})\Big]\\
\leq C\hat{\delta}^{1/2}.\end{split}

(8.8)

On the other hand, if
\vec{\eta}\in\textsc{goodflips}
, then Lemma
16
tells us that

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{|q^{\sigma_{\vec{\eta}}}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma_{\vec{\eta}}}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.

Consequently,

\begin{split}\text{E}_{a}\Big[\Big\{\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu}&)|^{2}]\Delta t_{\nu}\Big\}\\
&\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\textsc{goodflips}}(\vec{\xi})\Big]\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}.\end{split}

(8.9)

We learn from (
8.8
) and (
8.9
) that

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\Big]<\varepsilon.

(8.10)

Thus, we have shown that (
8.1
), (
8.2
) and
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}<\delta
imply (
8.10
).

That is, Lemma
16
holds without the assumption that
\sigma
is deterministic. From now on, when we apply that lemma, we needn’t check that
\sigma
is deterministic.

9
Reformulating the Main Bayesian Results

Let
\sigma
be a tame strategy.

Recall, from Section
9
, that
q^{\sigma}_{C}(t)=q^{\sigma}(t)
and
q^{\sigma}_{D}(t)=q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
for
t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1})
,
0\leq\nu<N
. Also,
u^{\sigma}(t)=u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})
for
t\in[t_{\nu},t_{\nu+1})
,
0\leq\nu<N
. Therefore,

\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}\\
=\int_{0}^{T}\{|u^{\sigma}(t)-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}+|q^{\sigma}_{D}(t)-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}_{D}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt

where
\tilde{\sigma}
is the
allegedly optimal strategy
for some Bayesian prior. Similarly,

\sum_{0\leq\nu<N}\{|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}+|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|^{2}\}\Delta t_{\nu}=\int_{0}^{T}\{|u^{\sigma}(t)|^{2}+|q^{\sigma}_{D}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt

and the analogous formula holds for
\tilde{\sigma}
.

From Section
9
, we have

\text{E}_{a}\big[\max_{t\in[0,T]}|q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\sigma}_{D}(t)|^{m}\big]\leq C_{m}(\Delta t_{\text{MAX}})^{m/4}\;\text{for any}\;m\geq 1

and any
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
; the analogous estimate holds for
\tilde{\sigma}
in place of
\sigma
.

In view of the above remarks, we can reformulate our main previous results, replacing
q^{\sigma}_{D}(t)
by
q^{\sigma}(t)
.

We define

\textsc{Cost}(\sigma)=\int_{0}^{T}\{(u^{\sigma}(t))^{2}+(q^{\sigma}(t))^{2}\}\ dt.

We combine the above discussion with Lemmas
3
,
6
,
14
, and
16
to deduce the following.

Theorem 4
.

[Main Theorem on Bayesian Strategies]
Let
\sigma
be a tame strategy, satisfying

|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|<C_{\emph{TAME}}^{\sigma}[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1].

Fix a Bayesian prior
d\emph{Prob}(a)
on
[-a_{\emph{MAX}},+a_{\emph{MAX}}]
, and suppose our PDE Assumption holds for the PDE arising from that prior. Let
\tilde{\sigma}
denote the
allegedly optimal strategy
for the same partition of
[0,T]
used to define
\sigma
.

Then given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
determined by
\varepsilon
, together with the
boilerplate constants
and the constant
C_{\emph{TAME}}^{\sigma}
, such that the following holds. Suppose
\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}}<\delta
. Then:

(1)

|\emph{E}[\textsc{Cost}(\tilde{\sigma})]-S(q_{0},0,0,0)|<\varepsilon,

where
S
is our PDE solution.

(2)

If
\emph{E}[\textsc{Cost}(\sigma)]<\emph{E}[\textsc{Cost}(\tilde{\sigma})]+\delta
, then for any
a\in[-a_{\emph{MAX}},+a_{\emph{MAX}}]
we have

\emph{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|u^{\sigma}(t)-u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}+|q^{\sigma}(t)-q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]<\varepsilon.

(3)

There exists an analytic function
I(a)
defined on
\mathcal{R}\equiv(-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}})\times(-\hat{c},\hat{c})
for some
\hat{c}
, such that
|I(a)|\leq C
on
\mathcal{R}
, and

|I(a)-\emph{E}_{a}[\textsc{Cost}(\sigma)]|<\varepsilon\;\text{for}\;a\in(-a_{\emph{MAX}},+a_{\emph{MAX}}).

(4)

If
a
exceeds a large enough constant
C
, then

\emph{E}_{a}[\textsc{Cost}(\sigma)]>cT^{2}\exp(caT).

Note that strictly speaking, we proved
(3)
and
(4)
for deterministic strategies, so they hold for
\sigma
once we condition on
\vec{\xi}=\vec{\eta}
for a fixed
\vec{\eta}\in\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
. Integrating over
\vec{\eta}
, we obtain
(3)
and
(4)
as stated.

10
Comparing the Allegedly Optimal Strategies for Two Partitions

Let
\pi
be a partition
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=T
, and let
\pi^{\prime}
be a refinement of
\pi
, given by
0=t_{0}^{\prime}<t_{1}^{\prime}<\dots<t_{N}^{\prime}=T
. Let
\tilde{\sigma},\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}
be the corresponding
allegedly optimal strategies
. We set
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}=\max_{\nu}(t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu})
and
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}^{\prime}=\max_{\nu}(t_{\nu+1}^{\prime}-t_{\nu}^{\prime})\leq\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}.
We will prove the following result.

Lemma 17
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
such that if
\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}}<\delta
, then

\emph{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)-q^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)-u^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]<\varepsilon

for all
a\in[-a_{\emph{MAX}},+a_{\emph{MAX}}]
.

Proof.

Given
\varepsilon>0
we pick
\hat{\delta}>0
small enough, then pick
\delta>0
small enough, depending on
\hat{\delta}
.

Suppose
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}<\delta
; then also
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}^{\prime}<\delta
. Theorem
4
gives

\Big|\text{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]-S(q_{0},0,0,0)\Big|<\hat{\delta}

(10.1)

and

\Big|\text{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]-S(q_{0},0,0,0)\Big|<\hat{\delta}.

(10.2)

In particular,

\text{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]<C

(10.3)

and

\text{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]<C.

(10.4)

Lemma
9
gives a tame strategy
\hat{\sigma}
associated to the partition
\pi^{\prime}
for which

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)-q^{\hat{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)-u^{\hat{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]\leq\hat{\delta}^{2}

(10.5)

for every
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
, hence

\text{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)-q^{\hat{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)-u^{\hat{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]\leq\hat{\delta}^{2}.

Together with (
10.3
), this implies that

\text{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\hat{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\hat{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]\leq\text{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]+C\hat{\delta}.

Thanks to (
10.1
) and (
10.2
), this in turn implies that

\text{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\hat{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\hat{\sigma}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]\leq\text{E}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]+C\hat{\delta}.

(10.6)

Recall that
\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}
is the
allegedly optimal strategy
associated to the partition
\pi^{\prime}
, while
\hat{\sigma}
is another tame strategy associated to
\pi^{\prime}
.

Consequently, by virtue of Theorem
4
, (
10.6
) implies that

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\hat{\sigma}}(t)-q^{\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\hat{\sigma}}(t)-u^{\hat{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]<\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

(10.7)

for every
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
.

From (
10.5
) and (
10.7
) we have

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)-q^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)-u^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]<\varepsilon,

completing the proof of the lemma.
∎

Corollary 4
.

Given
\varepsilon>0
there exists
\delta>0
for which the following holds.

Let

\pi:\;0=t_{0}=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=T

and

\pi^{\prime}:\;0=t_{0}^{\prime}<t_{1}^{\prime}<\dots<t_{N^{\prime}}^{\prime}=T

be partitions, let

\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}}=\max_{\nu}(t_{\nu+1}-t_{\nu})\quad\text{and}\quad\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}}^{\prime}=\max_{\nu}(t_{\nu+1}^{\prime}-t_{\nu}^{\prime}),

and let
\tilde{\sigma}
,
\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}
be the
allegedly optimal strategies
associated to
\pi
,
\pi^{\prime}
, respectively.

If
\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}},\Delta t_{\emph{MAX}}^{\prime}<\delta
, then

\emph{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)-q^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\tilde{\sigma}}(t)-u^{\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]<\varepsilon

for every
a\in[-a_{\emph{MAX}},+a_{\emph{MAX}}]
.

Proof.

Compare both
\tilde{\sigma}
and
\tilde{\sigma}^{\prime}
to the
allegedly optimal strategy
arising from a common refinement of
\pi
and
\pi^{\prime}
.
∎

Chapter 4
Decisions in Continuous Time

1
Tame Strategies with Decisions in Continuous Time

Suppose that for each
n=1,2,3,\dots
we are given a tame strategy
\sigma^{n}
associated to a partition
\pi^{n}
of the time interval
[0,T]
. Say
\pi^{n}
is given by

0=t_{0}^{n}<t_{1}^{n}<\dots<t_{N(n)}^{n}=T,

(1.1)

and
\sigma^{n}
is given by the collection of tame rules

\sigma^{n}=(\sigma^{n}_{t_{\nu}^{n}})_{\nu=0,1,\dots,N(n)-1},

where each
\sigma_{t_{\nu}^{n}}^{n}
is a function of
\nu
real variables
\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\nu}
and the coin flips
\vec{\xi}
.

We assume that

\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}^{n}:=\max_{\nu}(t_{\nu+1}^{n}-t_{\nu}^{n})\rightarrow 0\;\text{as}\;n\rightarrow\infty,

(1.2)

and that

|\sigma_{t_{\nu}^{n}}^{n}(\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\nu},\vec{\xi})|\leq C_{\text{TAME}}^{\vec{\sigma}}[|\bar{q}_{\nu}|+1]\;\text{for all}\;\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\nu}

(1.3)

for all
n,\nu
, with
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\vec{\sigma}}
independent of
n,\nu,\bar{q}_{1},\dots,\bar{q}_{\nu}
.

Each
\sigma^{n}
gives rise to control trajectories and particle trajectories
u^{\sigma^{n},a}(t)
and
q^{\sigma^{n},a}(t)
, respectively, for
t\in[0,T]
, once we specify that
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
. Here,
a
is an arbitrary real number, not necessarily belonging to
[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
. We define the
expected cost
of
\sigma^{n}
by

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{n},a)=\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{(u^{\sigma^{n},a}(t))^{2}+(q^{\sigma^{n},a}(t))^{2}\}\ dt\Big].

We say that
(\sigma^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a
Cauchy sequence of uniformly tame strategies
if (
1.2
) and (
1.3
) hold, and

\lim_{n,m\rightarrow\infty}\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|u^{\sigma^{n},a}(t)-u^{\sigma^{m},a}(t)|^{2}+|q^{\sigma^{n},a}(t)-q^{\sigma^{m},a}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]=0,

(1.4)

uniformly for
a
in any bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}
.

If
(\sigma^{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(\hat{\sigma}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
are two Cauchy sequences of uniformly tame strategies, then we call those sequences
equivalent
if we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|u^{\sigma^{n},a}(t)-u^{\hat{\sigma}^{n},a}(t)|^{2}+|q^{\sigma^{n},a}(t)-q^{\hat{\sigma}^{n},a}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]=0

(1.5)

for each
a\in\mathbb{R}
.

If
(\sigma^{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a Cauchy sequence of uniformly tame strategies, then for each
a\in\mathbb{R}
there exist random functions
u^{a}(t)
,
q^{a}(t)
s.t.

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|u^{\sigma^{n},a}(t)-u^{a}(t)|^{2}+|q^{\sigma^{n},a}(t)-q^{a}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]=0,

(1.6)

uniformly for
a
in any bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}
.

Moreover, if two Cauchy sequences
(\sigma^{n})_{n\geq 1}
,
(\hat{\sigma}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
are equivalent, then for each
a\in\mathbb{R}
, the
u^{a}
,
q^{a}
defined by those sequences are equal, for a.e.
t\in[0,T]
, almost surely with respect to
\text{Prob}_{a}
.

We define a
tame strategy
(for decisions in continuous time) to be an equivalence class of Cauchy sequences of uniformly tame strategies, with respect to the equivalence relation (
1.5
). We denote a tame strategy by
\vec{\sigma}=[[(\sigma^{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
, and we say that
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\vec{\sigma}}
in (
1.3
) is a
tame constant
for
\vec{\sigma}
. If
\vec{\sigma}=[[(\sigma^{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
is a tame strategy, then we write
q^{\vec{\sigma},a}(t)
and
u^{\vec{\sigma},a}(t)
to denote the functions
q^{a}(t)
,
u^{a}(t)
in (
1.6
).

If
\vec{\sigma}=[[(\sigma^{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
is a tame strategy, then we define

\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{n},a).

This quantity is well-defined, since the limit exists and two equivalent Cauchy sequences produce the same expected cost. Immediately from our results on tame strategies associated to partitions of
[0,T]
, we have the following results, for any tame strategy
\vec{\sigma}=[[(\sigma^{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
.

Note that

\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)=\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{(q^{\vec{\sigma},a}(t))^{2}+(u^{\vec{\sigma},a}(t))^{2}\}\ dt\Big]

for any tame strategy
\vec{\sigma}
and any
a\in\mathbb{R}
. For large enough
a>0
, we have

\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)\geq cT^{2}\exp(caT).

(1.7)

Moreover, we will see that the function

\begin{split}\parbox{200.0003pt}{$[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]\ni a\mapsto\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)$\\
continues to a bounded analytic function on\\
$(-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}})+i(-\hat{c},\hat{c})$
for some $\hat{c}>0$ determined by the {boilerplate constants} and the constant $C_{\text{TAME}}^{\vec{\sigma}}$.}\end{split}

(1.8)

Moreover, we may replace
a_{\text{MAX}}
by any
\hat{a}_{\text{MAX}}>a_{\text{MAX}}
, and the assumptions of the preceding sections are still valid; the constants determined by the
boilerplate constants
will now depend on
\hat{a}_{\text{MAX}}
. In particular, (
1.8
) immediately implies that the function

[-\hat{a}_{\text{MAX}},+\hat{a}_{\text{MAX}}]\ni a\mapsto\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)

continues to a bounded analytic function on

(-\hat{a}_{\text{MAX}},+\hat{a}_{\text{MAX}})+i(-\hat{c}(\hat{a}_{\text{MAX}}),+\hat{c}(\hat{a}_{\text{MAX}}));

the bound for that analytic function depends on
\hat{a}_{\text{MAX}}
. This implies that the function

\mathbb{R}\ni a\mapsto\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)

continues to an analytic function on a neighborhood of the real axis in
\mathbb{C}
. In other words,

\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)\;\text{is a real-analytic function of}\;a\in\mathbb{R}.

(1.9)

Let us check our assertion (
1.8
). Recall our previous result on analytic continuation:

Given
\varepsilon>0
, there exists
\delta>0
s.t. whenever
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}^{n}<\delta
we have

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{n},a)=I^{n}(a)+\text{ERROR}^{n}(a)

(1.10)

on
(-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}})
, where

|\text{ERROR}^{n}(a)|<\varepsilon\;\text{for}\;a\in(-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}),

(1.11)

and

I^{n}(a)\;\text{is analytic on }\mathcal{R}\equiv(-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}})+i(-\hat{c},\hat{c}),

(1.12)

with
|I^{n}(a)|\leq C
everywhere on that rectangle. In particular, (
1.10
), (
1.11
), (
1.12
) hold for all large enough
n
, since
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}^{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

Since the
I^{n}(a)
are uniformly bounded analytic functions on the rectangle
\mathcal{R}
, we may pick out a subsequence
I^{n_{j}}(a)
that converges to a bounded analytic function
I^{\infty}(a)
uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathcal{R}
. Applying (
1.10
) and (
1.11
) to
\sigma^{n_{j}}
, and passing to the limit as
j\rightarrow\infty
, we find that
\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)=I^{\infty}(a)
for all
a\in(-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}})
, completing the proof of (
1.8
).

Next, we construct an
allegedly optimal strategy
with
decisions in continuous time
.

Fix a prior probability distribution
d\text{Prob}(a)
on
[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Given
\vec{\sigma}=[[(\sigma_{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
we define

\displaystyle\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{n})=\int_{-a_{\text{MAX}}}^{a_{\text{MAX}}}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{n},a)\ d\text{Prob}(a),

\displaystyle\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma})=\int_{-a_{\text{MAX}}}^{a_{\text{MAX}}}\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)\ d\text{Prob}(a).

Let
\pi^{n}
be a sequence of partitions of
[0,T]
(as in (
1.1
)), with
\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}^{n}\rightarrow 0
as
n\rightarrow\infty
.

For each
n
, let
\tilde{\sigma}^{n}
denote the allegedly optimal strategy associated to the partition
\pi^{n}
. Corollary
4
tells us that
(\tilde{\sigma}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
satisfies condition (
1.4
). Moreover, we have assumed condition (
1.2
), and our PDE Assumption (see (
3.6
)) tells us that (
1.3
) holds. Thus, the
(\tilde{\sigma}^{n})_{n\geq 1}
form a Cauchy sequence of uniformly tame strategies.

We write
\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}}=[[(\tilde{\sigma}^{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
to denote the resulting continuous tame strategy. Note that
\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}}
is independent of the sequence of partitions used to define it. We will show that it is optimal for Bayesian control.

Let
\vec{\sigma}=[[(\sigma^{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
be a tame strategy with tame constant
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\vec{\sigma}}
. Then

\displaystyle\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma})=

\displaystyle\int_{-a_{\text{MAX}}}^{a_{\text{MAX}}}\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)\ d\text{Prob}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{-a_{\text{MAX}}}^{a_{\text{MAX}}}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{n},a)\ d\text{Prob}(a)

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{n}).

Here, the interchange of limit and integral is justified by the uniform convergence in
a
that we assumed in our definition of Cauchy sequences.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be given. For
n
large enough, condition (
1.2
) for the
\sigma^{n}
allows us to apply Theorem
4
; we conclude that

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{n})\geq\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma}^{n})-\varepsilon

for
n
large enough. Here,
\tilde{\sigma}^{n}
denotes the
allegedly optimal strategy
associated to the partition relevant to
\sigma^{n}
.

Passing to the limit as
n\rightarrow\infty
, we see that

\textsc{ECost}([[(\sigma^{n})_{n\geq 1}]])\geq\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}}).

Thus, indeed,
\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}}
is the optimal tame Bayesian strategy; any competing tame Bayesian strategy has an expected cost at least that of
\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}}
.

Next, we compare the cost of
\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}}
with that of another tame strategy
\vec{\sigma}=[[(\sigma_{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
conditioned on
a_{\text{TRUE}}=a
for a given
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
.

We will prove the following assertion:

\begin{split}&\parbox{200.0003pt}{Given $\varepsilon>0$ there exists $\delta$, depending on the {boilerplate constants} and the constant $C_{\text{TAME}}^{\vec{\sigma}}$, such that if}\\
&\qquad\qquad\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma})\leq\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}})+\delta,\\
&\text{then, for every}\;a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}],\text{we have}\\
&\qquad\qquad|\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)-\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}},a)|<\varepsilon.\end{split}

(1.13)

To prove (
1.13
), we recall that
\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}}=[[(\tilde{\sigma}_{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
with
\tilde{\sigma}_{n}
the allegedly optimal strategy associated to the partition associated to
\sigma_{n}
. Then

\displaystyle\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{n})

(1.14)

\displaystyle\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}})=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma}_{n})

(1.15)

\displaystyle\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{n},a)

(1.16)

\displaystyle\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}},a)=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma}_{n},a).

(1.17)

If
\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma})\leq\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}})+\delta
, then by (
1.14
), (
1.15
) we have

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{n})\leq\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma}_{n})+2\delta\;\text{for large enough}\;n.

Also, for large enough
n
, the partition of
[0,T]
associated to
\sigma_{n}
,
\tilde{\sigma}_{n}
has mesh less than
2\delta
. It therefore follows from Theorem
4
that

|\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{n},a)-\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma}_{n},a)|\leq\frac{\varepsilon}{2}

for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
and all large enough
n
.

From (
1.16
), (
1.17
) we now see that

|\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},a)-\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{\text{opt}},a)|\leq\varepsilon

for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
, completing the proof of (
1.13
).

2
Not-Necessarily-Tame Strategies

In this section we drop the restriction to tame strategies.

Let
\vec{\sigma}_{1},\vec{\sigma}_{2},\vec{\sigma}_{3},\dots
be tame strategies in the sense of Section
1
. We do
not
assume the
\vec{\sigma}_{n}
have a tame constant independent of
n
.

We say that the sequence
(\vec{\sigma}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is
Cauchy
if

\lim_{m,n\rightarrow\infty}\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\vec{\sigma}_{n},a}(t)-q^{\vec{\sigma}_{m},a}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\vec{\sigma}_{n},a}(t)-u^{\vec{\sigma}_{m},a}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]=0,

uniformly for
a
in any bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}
. Two Cauchy sequences
(\vec{\sigma}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
and
(\vec{\sigma}_{n}^{\#})_{n\geq 1}
will be called
equivalent
if

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\vec{\sigma}_{n},a}(t)-q^{\vec{\sigma}^{\#}_{n},a}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\vec{\sigma}_{n},a}(t)-u^{\vec{\sigma}^{\#}_{n},a}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]=0

for each
a\in\mathbb{R}
.

To a Cauchy sequence
(\vec{\sigma}_{n})_{n\geq 1}
as above, we associate the trajectories
q^{a}(t)
,
u^{a}(t)
for which we have

\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{|q^{\vec{\sigma}_{n},a}(t)-q^{a}(t)|^{2}+|u^{\vec{\sigma}_{n},a}(t)-u^{a}(t)|^{2}\}\ dt\Big]=0.

Two equivalent Cauchy sequences yield the same
q^{a}
and
u^{a}
. We define a
strategy
to be an equivalence class of Cauchy sequences under the above equivalence relation. We denote strategies by
\vec{\vec{\sigma}}=[[(\vec{\sigma}_{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
, and we write
q^{\vec{\vec{\sigma}},a}(t)
,
u^{\vec{\vec{\sigma}},a}(t)
, respectively, to denote the above functions
q^{a}(t)
,
u^{a}(t)
. We define

\begin{split}\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\vec{\sigma}},a)&=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{n},a)\\
&=\text{E}_{a}\Big[\int_{0}^{T}\{(q^{\vec{\vec{\sigma}},a}(t))^{2}+(u^{\vec{\vec{\sigma}},a}(t))^{2}\}\ dt\Big].\end{split}

These limits converge uniformly for
a
in any bounded subset of
\mathbb{R}
. Note that every tame strategy
\vec{\sigma}
may also be regarded as a strategy as defined just above, namely the equivalence class associated with the constant sequence
\vec{\sigma},\vec{\sigma},\vec{\sigma},\dots
.

Suppose we are given a prior probability distribution
d\text{Prior}
on the interval
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. We define

\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\vec{\sigma}},d\text{Prior})=\int_{-a_{\text{MAX}}}^{a_{\text{MAX}}}\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\vec{\sigma}},a)\ d\text{Prior}(a).

If
\vec{\vec{\sigma}}=[[(\vec{\sigma}_{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
, then

\displaystyle\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\vec{\sigma}},d\text{Prior})

\displaystyle=\int_{-a_{\text{MAX}}}^{a_{\text{MAX}}}\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{n},a)\ d\text{Prior}(a)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\int_{-a_{\text{MAX}}}^{a_{\text{MAX}}}\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{n},a)\ d\text{Prior}(a)

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{n},d\text{Prior});

the interchange of limit and integral is justified by the uniform convergence noted above.

Now let
\vec{\sigma}_{\text{Bayes}}(d\text{Prior})
be the optimal Bayesian strategy for
d\text{Prior}
, given in Section
1
. For any tame strategy
\vec{\sigma}
, we have seen that

\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma},d\text{Prior})\geq\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{\text{Bayes}}(d\text{Prior}),d\text{Prior}).

In particular, if
\vec{\vec{\sigma}}=[[(\vec{\sigma}_{n})_{n\geq 1}]]
, then

\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{n},d\text{Prior})\geq\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{\text{Bayes}}(d\text{Prior}),d\text{Prior}),

hence

\displaystyle\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\vec{\sigma}},d\text{Prior})

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{n},d\text{Prior})

\displaystyle\geq\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}_{\text{Bayes}}(d\text{Prior}),d\text{Prior}).

So we see that the strategy
\vec{\sigma}_{\text{Bayes}}(d\text{Prior})
has expected cost less than or equal to that of any competing strategy
\vec{\vec{\sigma}}
.

From now on, we drop the arrows from our notation. When we mention a strategy, we will make clear whether it is a general strategy, a tame strategy, or a tame strategy associated to a partition of
[0,T]
.

Combining the results of this Chapter with Theorem
4
, we deduce Theorems
1
and
2
from the introduction.

Chapter 5
Agnostic Control

Throughout this chapter the random variable
a_{\text{TRUE}}\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
is unknown and we
do not
assume that we have a prior belief about
a_{\text{TRUE}}
.

1
Mixed Strategies

Let

\phi:(\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\dots)\mapsto(\xi_{1},\xi_{3},\xi_{5},\dots)

be the map from
\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}\rightarrow\{0,1\}^{\mathbb{N}}
that erases every other bit.

Fix a partition

0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{N}=T,

(1.1)

and let
\sigma=(\sigma_{t_{\nu}})_{0\leq\nu<N}
be a tame strategy associated to the partition (
1.1
). Since
\sigma
is tame, we have

|u^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|\leq C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma}\cdot[|q^{\sigma}(t_{\nu})|+1]\;\text{for each}\;\nu.

(1.2)

We can pass from
\sigma
to the morally equivalent strategy

\sigma^{\#}=(\sigma^{\#}_{t_{\nu}})_{0\leq\nu<N}

by setting

\sigma_{t_{\nu}}^{\#}(q_{1},\dots,q_{\nu},\vec{\xi})=\sigma_{t_{\nu}}(q_{1},\dots,q_{\nu},\phi(\vec{\xi})).

Thus,
\sigma^{\#}
does precisely what
\sigma
does, except that whereas
\sigma
makes use of the bits
\xi_{1},\xi_{2},\xi_{3},\dots
,
\sigma^{\#}
makes use only of the bits
\xi_{1},\xi_{3},\xi_{5},\dots
.

Now let
\sigma^{0}=(\sigma_{t_{\nu}}^{0})_{0\leq\nu<N}
and
\sigma^{1}=(\sigma_{t_{\nu}}^{1})_{0\leq\nu<N}
be two tame strategies, both associated to the partition (
1.1
), and let
\theta\in[0,1]
be given. We define a
mixed strategy

\sigma^{\theta}
, as follows. First, we pass from
\sigma^{0}
and
\sigma^{1}
to the strategies
\sigma^{0\#}
,
\sigma^{1\#}
as above. These strategies make use of the bits
\xi_{1},\xi_{3},\xi_{5},\dots
but ignore the bits
\xi_{2},\xi_{4},\xi_{6},\dots
. We regard
\xi_{2},\xi_{4},\xi_{6},\dots
as the binary digits of a random variable
Y
taking values in
[0,1]
. If
Y\leq\theta
then we play the strategy
\sigma^{1\#}
at all times
t_{\nu}
. If instead
Y>\theta
, then we play the strategy
\sigma^{0\#}
at all times
t_{\nu}
.

Evidently,

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{0\#},a)=\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{0},a)

and

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{1\#},a)=\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{1},a)

for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
; and

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\theta},a)=\theta\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{1},a)+(1-\theta)\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{0},a)

(1.3)

for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, since
Y\leq\theta
with probability
\theta
. Note that
\sigma^{\theta}
is a tame strategy, with

C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma^{\theta}}\leq\max\{C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma^{0}},C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma^{1}}\}.

(1.4)

We have defined the intermediate strategy
\sigma^{\theta}
when
\sigma^{0}
and
\sigma^{1}
are tame strategies associated to the same partition (
1.1
) of
[0,T]
.

We next extend our definition to tame strategies with decisions in continuous time. Fix a sequence
\pi_{1},\pi_{2},\dots
of partitions of
[0,T]
, with
\text{mesh}(\pi_{i})\rightarrow 0
as
i\rightarrow\infty
. Let
\theta\in[0,1]
be given. Let
\sigma_{1}^{0},\sigma_{2}^{0},\sigma_{3}^{0},\dots
and
\sigma_{1}^{1},\sigma_{2}^{1},\sigma_{3}^{1},\dots
be tame strategies, where, for each
i
, the strategies
\sigma_{i}^{0}
and
\sigma_{i}^{1}
are associated to the partition
\pi_{i}
of
[0,T].

Suppose that
(\sigma_{i}^{0})_{i=1,2,\dots}
and
(\sigma_{i}^{1})_{i=1,2,\dots}
are Cauchy sequences, in the sense of Section
1
. Thus,
\vec{\sigma}^{0}=[[(\sigma_{i}^{0})_{i\geq 1}]]
and
\vec{\sigma}^{1}=[[(\sigma_{i}^{1})_{i\geq 1}]]
are tame strategies in the sense of that section. For each
i
, we pass from
\sigma_{i}^{0}
,
\sigma_{i}^{1}
to the mixed strategy
\sigma_{i}^{\theta}
associated to the partition
\pi_{i}
of
[0,T]
. Then
\sigma_{1}^{\theta}
,
\sigma_{2}^{\theta},\dots
is again a Cauchy sequence in the sense of Section
1
. We write
\vec{\sigma}^{\theta}
to denote the tame strategy
[[(\sigma_{i}^{\theta})_{i\geq 1}]]
.
Then we have

\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}^{\theta},a)=\theta\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}^{1},a)+(1-\theta)\textsc{ECost}(\vec{\sigma}^{0},a)

for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, and

C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma_{i}^{\theta}}\leq\max\{C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma_{i}^{0}},C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma_{i}^{1}}\},

as follows easily from (
1.3
) and (
1.4
).

Note that we have restricted attention to tame strategies
[[(\sigma_{i}^{0})_{i\geq 1}]]
and
[[(\sigma_{i}^{1})_{i\geq 1}]]
in which, for each
i
,
\sigma_{i}^{0}
and
\sigma_{i}^{1}
are associated to the same partition of
[0,T]
. It would be natural to dispense with this restriction, but for our purposes that won’t be necessary.

2
Efficient Strategies are Bayesian

In this section we deal with tame strategies in the sense of Section
1
of Chapter
4
.

Suppose we are given a class of strategies, which we call the
legal strategies
.

Assume that given two
legal strategies

\sigma^{0}
and
\sigma^{1}
, and given
\theta\in[0,1]
, there exists a
legal strategy

\sigma^{\theta}
for which we have

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\theta},a)=(1-\theta)\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{0},a)+\theta\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{1},a)

(2.1)

for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
.

For example, suppose we fix a constant
\hat{C}
and a sequence of partitions
(\pi_{i})_{i\geq 1}
of
[0,T]
, with
\text{mesh}(\pi_{i})\rightarrow 0
as
i\rightarrow\infty
.

Then the class of all tame strategies
[[(\sigma_{i})_{i\geq 1}]]
with
\sigma_{i}
associated to
\pi_{i}
and
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma_{i}}\leq\hat{C}
satisfies (
2.1
), thanks to our discussion of mixed strategies in Section
1
.

Fix a finite set
A\subset[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, and let
\varepsilon\geq 0
be given. (Note that we allow
\varepsilon=0
.)

A
legal strategy

\sigma
will be said to be
efficient with tolerance
\varepsilon
if there does not exist another
legal strategy

\sigma^{\prime}
such that

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\prime},a)<\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a)-\varepsilon

(2.2)

for all
a\in A
. This notion depends on the set
A
and the class of
legal strategies
.

In this section we use a simple convexity argument to prove the following result.

Lemma 18

(Efficient Strategies are Bayesian)
.

Fix
A,\varepsilon
and a class of
legal strategies
as above, and let
\hat{\sigma}
be a
legal strategy
. Suppose
\hat{\sigma}
is efficient with tolerance
\varepsilon
. Then there exists a prior probability distribution
(p(a))_{a\in A}
such that for all other
legal strategies

\sigma^{\prime}
we have

\sum_{a\in A}p(a)\textsc{ECost}(\hat{\sigma},a)\leq\sum_{a\in A}p(a)\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\prime},a)+\varepsilon.

(2.3)

Proof.

For any strategy
\sigma
, define the
cost vector

\overrightarrow{\textsc{ECost}}(\sigma)
to be the vector
(\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a))_{a\in A}\in\mathbb{R}^{A}
. Thanks to (
2.1
), the set
\mathcal{K}
of all cost vectors of legal strategies is convex. Define another convex set
\mathcal{K}_{-}\subset\mathbb{R}^{A}
to consist of all vectors
(v_{a})_{a\in A}
such that
v_{a}<\textsc{ECost}(\hat{\sigma},a)-\varepsilon
for all
a\in A
. Because
\hat{\sigma}
is efficient with tolerance
\varepsilon
, the convex sets
\mathcal{K}
and
\mathcal{K}_{-}
are disjoint. Hence there exists a nonzero linear functional
\lambda:\mathbb{R}^{A}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
such that
\lambda(v)\leq\lambda(v^{*})
whenever
v\in\mathcal{K}_{-}
and
v^{*}\in\mathcal{K}
. The functional
\lambda
has the form

\lambda((v_{a})_{a\in A})=\sum_{a\in A}p(a)v_{a},

with at least one nonzero coefficient
p(a_{0})
. By definition of
\mathcal{K}_{-}
,
\mathcal{K}
, and
\lambda
, the following holds.

\begin{split}&\parbox{200.0003pt}{Let $\sigma^{\prime}$ be a {legal strategy}, and let $(v_{a})_{a\in A}$ satisfy}\\
&v_{a}<\textsc{ECost}(\hat{\sigma},a)-\varepsilon\text{ for all }a\in A.\\
&\text{Then}\\
&\sum_{a\in A}p(a)v_{a}\leq\sum_{a\in A}p(a)\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\prime},a).\end{split}

(2.4)

We claim that the
p(a)
are all nonnegative. Indeed, suppose
p(\hat{a})<0
for some
\hat{a}\in A
. We take
\sigma^{\prime}=\hat{\sigma}
,
v_{a}=\textsc{ECost}(\hat{\sigma},a)-\varepsilon-1
for
a\in A\backslash\{\hat{a}\}
, and
v_{\hat{a}}=-\mathcal{V}
for some large positive
\mathcal{V}
. If
\mathcal{V}
is large enough, then the above
\sigma^{\prime}
,
(v_{a})_{a\in A}
violate (
2.4
). So, as claimed, the
p(a)
are all nonnegative.

Since also the
p(a)
are not all zero, we may multiply the
p(a)
by a positive normalizing constant to preserve (
2.4
) and achieve also

\sum_{a\in A}p(a)=1.

(2.5)

Thus,
(p(a))_{a\in A}
is a probability distribution.

Now let
\delta>0
, and let
v_{a}=\textsc{ECost}(\hat{\sigma},a)-\varepsilon-\delta
for
a\in A
. Thanks to (
2.4
), (
2.5
) we have

\sum_{a\in A}p(a)\textsc{ECost}(\hat{\sigma},a)-\varepsilon-\delta\leq\sum_{a\in A}p(a)\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\prime},a)

for every legal strategy
\sigma^{\prime}
.

Since
\delta>0
may be taken arbitrarily small, inequality (
2.3
) follows, completing the proof of the Lemma.
∎

3
Regret

We fix continuous functions
\rho_{0},\rho_{1}:\mathbb{R}\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
. We suppose that

|\rho_{0}(a)|\leq\tilde{C}\;\text{and}\;0<\tilde{c}<\rho_{1}(a)<\tilde{C}\;\text{for}\;a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}].

For any strategy
\sigma
and any
a\in\mathbb{R}
, we define

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma,a)=\rho_{0}(a)+\rho_{1}(a)\cdot\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a).

We add the above
\tilde{c}
and
\tilde{C}
to our list of
boilerplate constants
. As usual,
c
,
C
,
C^{\prime}
, etc. denote constants depending only on the
boilerplate constants
. These symbols may denote different constants in different occurrences. The above notion of regret includes as special cases our earlier notions of additive, multiplicative, and hybrid regret.

4
The Main Lemma on Agnostic Control

Suppose our PDE Assumption holds (with the same constants
K
,
m_{0}
,
C^{\text{opt}}_{\text{TAME}}
) for every prior probability distribution on a given interval
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Under that assumption (see Section
3
), we prove the following result.

Lemma 19
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
, and let
A\subset[-a_{\emph{MAX}},a_{\emph{MAX}}]
be finite. Then there exist a subset
A_{0}\subset A
, a probability measure
\mu
, and a strategy
\tilde{\sigma}
, with the following properties.

(1)

The measure
\mu
is concentrated on
A_{0}
.

(2)

\tilde{\sigma}
is the optimal Bayesian strategy for the prior
\mu
.

(3)

For
a\in A
and
a_{0}\in A_{0}
, we have

\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a)\leq\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a_{0})+\varepsilon.

In particular,

(4)

|\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a_{0})-\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a_{0}^{\prime})|\leq\varepsilon
for
a_{0},a_{0}^{\prime}\in A_{0}
.

For the proof of the above lemma, we first fix a class of strategies that we call OK. For
i=1,2,3,\dots,
let
\sigma_{i}
be a tame strategy arising from the partition
[0,T]\cap 2^{-i}T\mathbbm{Z}
of the time interval
[0,T]
. Suppose that the
\sigma_{i}
form a Cauchy sequence in the sense of Section
1
, and that the tame constants
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\sigma_{i}}
are all less than or equal to the constant
C_{\text{TAME}}^{\text{opt}}
in our PDE Assumption (see Section
3
, inequality (
3.6
)). Then the strategy
\vec{\sigma}=[[(\sigma_{i})_{i=1,2,\dots}]]
will be called
OK
.

We make two crucial observations regarding OK strategies:

(I)

For any prior
\mu
on
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, the optimal Bayesian strategy
\tilde{\sigma}
is OK.

(II)

If
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}
are OK strategies, then so is the mixed strategy that plays strategy
\sigma
with probability
\theta
and strategy
\sigma^{\prime}
with probability
(1-\theta)
(for
0\leq\theta\leq 1
).

If
A
is any finite subset of
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
and
\sigma
is any strategy, we write
\text{MR}(\sigma,A)
to denote the quantity
\max\{\textsc{Regret}(\sigma,a):a\in A\}
. For any strategy
\sigma
and any prior
\mu
on a finite set
A
we write

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,\mu)=\sum_{a\in A}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a)\mu(a).

We now begin the proof of Lemma
19
.

Proof.

We proceed by induction on
\#A
, the number of elements of
A
.

In the base case
,
\#A=1
, i.e.,
A=\{a_{0}\}
for some
a_{0}\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. We take
A_{0}=A
,
\mu=\text{point mass at }a_{0}
,
\tilde{\sigma}=\text{optimal known-}a\text{ strategy for }a=a_{0}
. The conclusions of the lemma are obvious.

For the induction step
, we fix
k\geq 2
and assume the

induction hypothesis
: Our lemma holds whenever
\#A<k
.

We fix
A
with
\#A=k
, and prove the Lemma for
A
.

Let
\varepsilon>0
be given. We pick
\varepsilon_{0},\varepsilon_{1},\dots,\varepsilon_{7}>0
, with
\varepsilon_{0}=\varepsilon
; and with
\varepsilon_{i+1}
small enough, depending on
\varepsilon_{0},\dots,\varepsilon_{i}
and the
boilerplate constants
.

Let
\text{MR}_{*}=\inf\{\text{MR}(\sigma,A):\sigma\;\text{any OK strategy}\}
and let
\sigma_{*}
be an OK strategy such that

\text{MR}(\sigma_{*},A)\leq\text{MR}_{*}+\varepsilon_{7}.

For any other OK strategy
\sigma^{\prime}
, we have

\text{MR}(\sigma_{*},A)\leq\text{MR}(\sigma^{\prime},A)+\varepsilon_{7}.

(4.1)

If some OK strategy
\sigma^{\prime}
satisfied

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\prime},a)\leq\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{*},a)-C\varepsilon_{7}

for all
a\in A
and a large enough constant
C
, then
\sigma^{\prime}
would violate (
4.1
). Therefore,
\sigma_{*}
is
C\varepsilon_{7}
-efficient on
A
for the class of OK strategies. Thanks to observation
(II)
and Lemma
18
, there exists a probability measure
\mu
on
A
, such that

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{*},\mu)\leq\textsc{ECost}(\sigma^{\prime},\mu)+\varepsilon_{6}

(4.2)

for any OK strategy
\sigma^{\prime}
. In particular, (
4.2
) holds for the optimal Bayesian strategy for
\mu
, denoted
\tilde{\sigma}
. (Here we use observation
(I)
.) It therefore follows from Theorem
4
that

|\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{*},a)-\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma},a)|\leq\varepsilon_{5}\;\text{for all}\;a\in A.

Together with (
4.1
), this shows that

\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)\leq\text{MR}(\sigma^{\prime},A)+C\varepsilon_{5}

(4.3)

for all OK strategies
\sigma^{\prime}
. It may happen that

\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a)\geq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\varepsilon_{3}\;\text{for all}\;a\in A.

(4.4)

In that case, the conclusions of our lemma hold for
\tilde{\sigma}
,
\mu
and
A_{0}=A
. Hence, we may assume that (
4.4
) is false. Let

A_{0}=\{a\in A:\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\varepsilon_{3}\leq\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a)\leq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},a)\}.

(4.5)

Thus,

\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\varepsilon_{3}\leq\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a)\leq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)\;\text{for}\;a\in A_{0}

(4.6)

and

\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a)<\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\varepsilon_{3}\;\text{for}\;a\in A\backslash A_{0}.

(4.7)

Since (
4.4
) is false, we have
\#A_{0}<\#A
, so our
inductive hypothesis
applies, i.e., our lemma holds for
A_{0}
.

Thus, there exist a subset
A_{00}\subset A_{0}
, a probability measure
\mu_{0}
, and a strategy
\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
, with the following properties.

\displaystyle\mu_{0}\;\text{is concentrated on }A_{00}.

(4.8)

\displaystyle\tilde{\sigma}_{0}\;\text{is the optimal Bayesian strategy for the prior }\mu_{0}.

(4.9)

\displaystyle\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a)\leq\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a_{0})+\varepsilon_{7}\;\text{for }a\in A_{0},a_{0}\in A_{00}.

(4.10)

In particular,

|\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a_{0})-\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a_{0}^{\prime})|\leq\varepsilon_{7}\;\text{for}\;a_{0},a_{0}^{\prime}\in A_{00}.

(4.11)

From (
4.10
), (
4.11
) we see that

\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})-\varepsilon_{6}\leq\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a_{0})\leq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})\;\text{for }a_{0}\in A_{00}.

(4.12)

Our plan is to prove that the conclusions of Lemma
19
for
A
hold for the set
A_{00}
, the measure
\mu_{0}
, and the strategy
\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
; that will complete our induction on
\#A
and prove Lemma
19
. To carry out our plan, we first prove that

|\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})|\leq\varepsilon_{2}.

(4.13)

To see (
4.13
), we recall that

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma,a)=\rho_{0}(a)+\rho_{1}(a)\cdot\textsc{ECost}(\sigma,a)

with
c<\rho_{1}(a)<C
.

For
a_{0}\in A_{00}\subset A_{0}
, estimates (
4.6
) and (
4.12
) therefore imply the inequalities

\Big[\frac{\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\rho_{0}(a)}{\rho_{1}(a)}\Big]-C\varepsilon_{3}\leq\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma},a)\leq\Big[\frac{\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\rho_{0}(a)}{\rho_{1}(a)}\Big]

(4.14)

and

\begin{split}\Big[\frac{\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})-\rho_{0}(a)}{\rho_{1}(a)}\Big]-C\varepsilon_{6}\leq\textsc{ECost}(&\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a)\\
&\leq\Big[\frac{\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})-\rho_{0}(a)}{\rho_{1}(a)}\Big].\end{split}

(4.15)

Let

H_{1}=\sum_{a\in A_{00}}\frac{\mu_{0}(a)}{\rho_{1}(a)},\qquad H_{0}=\sum_{a\in A_{00}}\frac{\mu_{0}(a)\rho_{0}(a)}{\rho_{1}(a)}.

(4.16)

Since
\mu_{0}
is a probability measure concentrated on
A_{00}
, and since
c<\rho_{1}(a)<C
, we have

c^{\prime}<H_{1}<C^{\prime}.

(4.17)

Multiplying (
4.14
), (
4.15
) by
\mu_{0}(a)
, and summing over
a\in A_{00}
, we obtain the inequalities

H_{1}\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-H_{0}-C\varepsilon_{3}\leq\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma},\mu_{0})\leq H_{1}\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-H_{0}

(4.18)

and

H_{1}\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})-H_{0}-C\varepsilon_{6}\leq\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},\mu_{0})\leq H_{1}\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})-H_{0}.

(4.19)

Moreover, since
\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
is the optimal Bayesian strategy for the prior
\mu_{0}
, we have

\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},\mu_{0})\leq\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma},\mu_{0}).

(4.20)

From (
4.18
), (
4.19
), (
4.20
) we see that

H_{1}\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})-H_{0}-C\varepsilon_{6}\leq\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},\mu_{0})\leq\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma},\mu_{0})\\
\leq H_{1}\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-H_{0}.

Thus,

H_{1}\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})\leq H_{1}\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)+C\varepsilon_{6}.

Thanks to (
4.17
), this tells us that

\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})\leq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)+C\varepsilon_{6}.

So we’ve proven half of (
4.13
). In particular, (
4.13
) holds unless we have

\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})\leq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\varepsilon_{2}.

(4.21)

To complete the proof of (
4.13
), we assume (
4.21
) and derive a contradiction as follows.

Observation
(I)
tells us that both strategies
\tilde{\sigma}
and
\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
are OK. We form a mixed strategy
\sigma_{\text{MIX}}
by playing the strategy
\tilde{\sigma}
with probability
(1-\varepsilon_{4})
and the strategy
\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
with probability
\varepsilon_{4}
. Observation
(II)
tells us that
\sigma_{\text{MIX}}
is an OK strategy. We will see that (
4.21
) implies that
\sigma_{\text{MIX}}
outperforms
\tilde{\sigma}
, contradicting (
4.3
). To see this, we first recall that since
\tilde{\sigma},\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
are tame strategies, we have
\textsc{Cost}(\tilde{\sigma},a)
,
\textsc{Cost}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a)\leq C
for all
a\in A
, hence

\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a)\leq\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a)+C\;\text{for any}\;a\in A.

(4.22)

Now suppose
a\in A_{0}
. Then (
4.21
) yields the inequalities

\displaystyle\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\text{MIX}},a)=

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon_{4})\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a)+\varepsilon_{4}\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon_{4})\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)+\varepsilon_{4}\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon_{4})\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)+\varepsilon_{4}[\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\varepsilon_{2}]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\varepsilon_{4}\varepsilon_{2}.

On the other hand, for
a\in A\backslash A_{0}
, inequalities (
4.7
) and (
4.22
) imply that

\displaystyle\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\text{MIX}},a)=

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon_{4})\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a)+\varepsilon_{4}\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle(1-\varepsilon_{4})[\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\varepsilon_{3}]+\varepsilon_{4}[\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)+C]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)+C\varepsilon_{4}-(1-\varepsilon_{4})\varepsilon_{3}\leq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{3}

(since
\varepsilon_{4}\ll\varepsilon_{3}\ll 1)
. Thus, for all
a\in A
, we have

\displaystyle\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\text{MIX}},a)

\displaystyle\leq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\min\{\frac{1}{2}\varepsilon_{3},\varepsilon_{4}\varepsilon_{2}\}

\displaystyle=\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\varepsilon_{2}\varepsilon_{4}.

In other words,

\text{MR}(\sigma_{\text{MIX}},A)\leq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-\varepsilon_{4}\varepsilon_{2}.

As promised, this contradicts (
4.3
), completing the proof of (
4.13
).

Returning to (
4.18
) and (
4.19
), we now see that

|\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma},\mu_{0})-\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},\mu_{0})|\leq C\varepsilon_{3},

(4.23)

thanks to (
4.13
) and (
4.17
).

Since
\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
is the optimal Bayesian strategy for
\mu_{0}
, and since
\tilde{\sigma}
is tame with tame constant at most
C
, (
4.23
) and Theorem
4
together imply that

|\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma},a)-\textsc{ECost}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a)|\leq\varepsilon_{1}\;\text{for all}\;a\in A.

(4.24)

We are ready to show that
A_{00}
,
\mu_{0}
,
\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
satisfy the conclusions of Lemma
19
for
A
. Indeed, we know that
\mu_{0}
is a probability measure concentrated on
A_{00}
, and that
\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
is the optimal Bayesian strategy for the prior
\mu_{0}
. It remains only to show that

\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a)\leq\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a_{0})+\varepsilon\;\text{for any}\;a_{0}\in A_{00},a\in A.

(4.25)

However, (
4.24
) yields

\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a)\leq\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma},a)+C\varepsilon_{1}\leq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)+C\varepsilon_{1}\;\text{for }a\in A,

(4.26)

while (
4.12
) and (
4.13
) yield

\textsc{Regret}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},a_{0})\geq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma}_{0},A_{0})-\varepsilon_{6}\geq\text{MR}(\tilde{\sigma},A)-C\varepsilon_{2}

(4.27)

for
a_{0}\in A_{00}
.

The desired estimate (
4.25
) is immediate from (
4.26
) and (
4.27
). Thus, as promised, the conclusions of our lemma hold, with
A_{00}
,
\mu_{0}
,
\tilde{\sigma}_{0}
in place of
A_{0}
,
\mu
,
\tilde{\sigma}
. Our induction on
\#A
is complete, and Lemma
19
is proven.
∎

5
An Interval of Allowed Parameters

The previous section produced nearly optimal agnostic strategies when the parameter
a
is known to belong to a finite set. In this section we pass to the case in which
a
is known merely to belong to a given interval
[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
. We continue to suppose our PDE Assumption (from Section
3
) holds for every prior probability distribution on the interval
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Under this assumption, we will prove the following result.

Theorem 5
.

There exists a Bayesian prior probability measure
\mu_{\infty}
supported on a subset
A_{\infty}\subset[-a_{\emph{MAX}},+a_{\emph{MAX}}]
, for which the optimal Bayesian strategy
\sigma_{\infty}
satisfies

(A)

The function
a\mapsto\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
is constant on
A_{\infty}
, and

(B)

The function
[-a_{\emph{MAX}},a_{\emph{MAX}}]\ni a\mapsto\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
is maximized on
A_{\infty}
.

Proof.

Let
A_{1},A_{2},A_{3},\dots
be a sequence of sets of the form

\displaystyle A_{N}=[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]\cap 2^{-m_{N}}\mathbbm{Z},\text{ with }m_{N}\rightarrow\infty\text{ as }N\rightarrow\infty;\text{ and let}

(5.1)

\displaystyle\varepsilon_{1},\varepsilon_{2},\varepsilon_{3},\dots\text{ be a sequence of positive numbers tending to zero.}

(5.2)

Applying Lemma
19
to each
A_{N}
, we obtain a probability measure
\mu_{N}
, concentrated on a subset
A_{N}^{0}\subset A_{N}
, such that the optimal Bayesian strategy
\sigma_{N}
for the prior
\mu_{N}
satisfies

\displaystyle\text{MR}_{N}-\varepsilon_{N}\leq\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a^{0})\leq\text{MR}_{N}\;\text{for all }a^{0}\in A_{N}^{0},\text{ where}

(5.3)

\displaystyle\text{MR}_{N}=\max\{\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a):a\in A_{N}\}.

(5.4)

Passing to a subsequence, we may assume that the
\mu_{N}
converge weakly to a probability measure
\mu_{\infty}
on
[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Again passing to a subsequence, we may assume that the
\text{MR}_{N}
converge to a limit
\text{MR}_{\infty}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. (Here we use the fact that the
\text{MR}_{N}
are bounded, thanks to Lemma
1
.)

Let
\sigma_{\infty}
be the optimal Bayesian strategy for the prior
\mu_{\infty}
. After again passing to a subsequence, we will show that

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a)\rightarrow\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)\;\text{as}\;N\rightarrow\infty,

(5.5)

uniformly for
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},+a_{\text{MAX}}]
. The proof of (
5.5
