) is the main step in our argument.

Let us recall how
\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a)
and
\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
are defined. Starting from the prior
\mu_{N}
, we form the functions

\bar{a}_{N}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=\frac{\int_{-a_{\text{MAX}}}^{a_{\text{MAX}}}a\exp\Big(-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\zeta_{2}+a\zeta_{1}\Big)\ d\mu_{N}(a)}{\int_{-a_{\text{MAX}}}^{a_{\text{MAX}}}\exp\Big(-\frac{a^{2}}{2}\zeta_{2}+a\zeta_{1}\Big)\ d\mu_{N}(a)},

and similarly define
\bar{a}_{\infty}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
.

Using
\bar{a}_{N}
in place of
\bar{a}
in equation (
3.1
), we then obtain a PDE solution
S_{N}(q,t,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})\in C_{loc}^{2,1}(\mathbb{R}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)),
satisfying the conditions given in Section
3
. Thanks to the estimates on
\partial^{\alpha}S_{N}
(
|\alpha|\leq 3)
in that section, we may again pass to a subsequence, and assume that

\partial^{\alpha}S_{N}\rightarrow\partial^{\alpha}S_{\infty}\;\text{as}\;N\rightarrow\infty\;\text{for}\;|\alpha|\leq 2,

(5.6)

uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
. Here,
S_{\infty}\in C_{loc}^{2,1}
satisfies all the estimates given in Section
3
. Since the
\mu_{N}
converge weakly to
\mu_{\infty}
, it follows that
\bar{a}_{N}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})\rightarrow\bar{a}_{\infty}(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})
as
N\rightarrow\infty
for each
(\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
. Together with (
5.6
) and the PDE satisfied by the
S_{N}
, this proves that
S_{\infty}
satisfies the PDE (
3.1
) for the Bayesian prior
\mu_{\infty}
.

Now define

u_{N}(q,t,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}S_{N}(q,t,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})

and

u_{\infty}(q,t,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})=-\frac{1}{2}\partial_{q}S_{\infty}(q,t,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})

for
(q,t,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})\in\mathbb{R}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
. From (
5.6
) we have

u_{N}\rightarrow u_{\infty}\;\text{as}\;N\rightarrow\infty,

(5.7)

uniformly on compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}\times[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times[0,\infty)
.

For each
k\geq 1
, we introduce the partition
\pi_{k}
of
[0,T]
given by

0=t_{0}^{k}<t_{1}^{k}<\dots<t_{k}^{k}=T,\;\text{with}\;t_{\nu}^{k}=\frac{\nu}{k}T.

(5.8)

Let
\sigma(N,k)
be the
allegedly optimal strategy
for the Bayesian prior
\mu_{N}
and the partition
\pi_{k}
. Thus,

u^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k})=u_{N}(q^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}),t_{\nu}^{k},\zeta_{1}^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}),\zeta_{2}^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}))\;\text{for}\;0\leq\nu<k.

(5.9)

Similarly, let
\sigma(\infty,k)
be the
allegedly optimal strategy
for the Bayesian prior
\mu_{\infty}
and the partition
\pi_{k}
. Thus,

\begin{split}u^{\sigma(\infty,k)}(&t_{\nu}^{k})\\
&=u_{\infty}(q^{\sigma(\infty,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}),t_{\nu}^{k},\zeta_{1}^{\sigma(\infty,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}),\zeta_{2}^{\sigma(\infty,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}))\;\text{for}\;0\leq\nu<k.\end{split}

(5.10)

By definition,

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{N},a)=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma(N,k),a)

(5.11)

and

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{\infty},a)=\lim_{k\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{ECost}(\sigma(\infty,k),a),

(5.12)

for
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Finally,

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a)=\rho_{0}(a)+\rho_{1}(a)\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{N},a)

(5.13)

and

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)=\rho_{0}(a)+\rho_{1}(a)\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{\infty},a),

(5.14)

for
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. This concludes our review of the definition of
\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a)
and
\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
.

For large enough
N
and
k
, we will apply Lemma
15
of Section
6
to the Bayesian prior
\mu_{\infty}
, the
allegedly optimal strategy

\sigma(\infty,k)
, and the alternative strategy
\sigma(N,k)
. Thus,
u_{\infty}
will play the rôle of
u_{\text{opt}}
in Section
6
, while
u^{\sigma(N,k)}
, given by (
5.9
), will play the rôle of
u^{\sigma}
in Section
6
. The rôle of the quantity
\textsc{Discrep}^{\sigma}
in Section
6
will therefore be played by

\begin{split}\textsc{Discrep}(N,k,t_{\nu}^{k})=u_{N}&(q^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}),t_{\nu}^{k},\zeta_{1}^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}),\zeta_{2}^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}))\\
&-u_{\infty}(q^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}),t_{\nu}^{k},\zeta_{1}^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k}),\zeta_{2}^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k})).\end{split}

(5.15)

To apply Lemma
15
, we must estimate

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<k}|\textsc{Discrep}(N,k,t_{\nu}^{k})|^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}^{k}\Big]\;\text{for}\;a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}],

(5.16)

with
\Delta t_{\nu}^{k}=t_{\nu+1}^{k}-t_{\nu}^{k}=T/k
(see (
5.8
)). To estimate the quantity in (
5.16
), we recall that

|u_{N}(q,t,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})|,|u_{\infty}(q,t,\zeta_{1},\zeta_{2})|\leq C[|q|+1],

and, consequently,

|\textsc{Discrep}(N,k,t_{\nu}^{k})|\leq C[|q^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{*})|+1].

(5.17)

For
Q\geq C
, define the events

\textsc{bad}(N,k,Q):=\big\{\max_{0\leq\nu<k}\{|q^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k})|+|\zeta_{1}^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k})|+|\zeta_{2}^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k})|\}>Q\big\}

and

\textsc{good}(N,k,Q):=\big\{\max_{0\leq\nu<k}\{|q^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k})|+|\zeta_{1}^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k})|+|\zeta_{2}^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k})|\}\leq Q\big\}.

We take
k\geq C
for a large
C
, so that our partition of
[0,T]
is fine enough to allow us to apply Lemma
1
. Lemma
1
then tells us that

\text{E}_{a}\big[\max_{0\leq\nu<k}\{|q^{\sigma(N,k)}(t_{\nu}^{k})|+1\}^{2}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\textsc{bad}(N,k,Q)}\big]\leq CQ^{-1}

for
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Hence, by (
5.17
),

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<k}(\textsc{Discrep}(N,k,t_{\nu}^{k}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}^{k}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\textsc{bad}(N,k,Q)}\Big]\leq CQ^{-1}

(5.18)

for
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. On the other hand, let
\delta>0
be given, and suppose

N\geq N_{\min}(\delta,Q)\;\text{for a large enough}\;N_{\min}(\delta,Q).

Then, by comparing (
5.7
) with (
5.15
), we see that

|\textsc{Discrep}(N,k,t_{\nu}^{k})|\leq\delta\;\text{for all}\;\nu,

provided
\textsc{good}(N,k,Q)
occurs. Therefore,

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<k}(\textsc{Discrep}(N,k,t_{\nu}^{k}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}^{k}\cdot\mathbbm{1}_{\textsc{good}(N,k,Q)}\Big]\leq C\delta

for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Together with (
5.18
), this implies that

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<k}(\textsc{Discrep}(N,k,t_{\nu}^{k}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}^{k}\Big]\leq C\delta+CQ^{-1}

for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
provided
N\geq N_{\min}(\delta,Q)
and
k\geq C
. Taking
Q=\delta^{-1}
, we have

\text{E}_{a}\Big[\sum_{0\leq\nu<k}(\textsc{Discrep}(N,k,t_{\nu}^{k}))^{2}\Delta t_{\nu}^{k}\Big]\leq C\delta

(5.19)

for
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
,
N\geq N_{\min}^{\prime}(\delta)
, and
k\geq C
. Also, recalling (
5.8
), we see that

\Delta t_{\text{MAX}}^{k}\equiv\max_{0\leq\nu<k}(t_{\nu+1}^{k}-t_{\nu}^{k})\leq\frac{C}{k}<\delta\;\text{provided}\;k>\frac{C}{\delta}.

(5.20)

Now let
\varepsilon>0
be given, and let
\delta
be small enough, depending on
\varepsilon
. Our results (
5.19
) and (
5.20
) are the hypotheses of Lemma
15
, with
\mu_{\infty}
in place of
d\text{Prior}
, and with
\sigma(N,k)
in place of
\sigma
. Applying that lemma, we learn that

|\textsc{ECost}(\sigma(N,k),a)-\textsc{ECost}(\sigma(\infty,k),a)|\leq\varepsilon,

all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
, for
k\geq k_{\min}(\varepsilon)
and
N\geq N^{\prime\prime}_{\min}(\varepsilon)
. Passing to the limit as
k\rightarrow\infty
for fixed
N
, and recalling (
5.11
) and (
5.12
), we see that

|\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{N},a)-\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{\infty},a)|\leq\varepsilon

for
N\geq N^{\prime\prime}(\varepsilon)
and for all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Since
\varepsilon>0
is arbitrary, we conclude that

\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{N},a)\rightarrow\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{\infty},a)\;\text{as}\;N\rightarrow\infty,

uniformly for
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Thanks to (
5.13
) and (
5.14
), this in turn implies that

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a)\rightarrow\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)\;\text{as}\;N\rightarrow\infty,

uniformly for
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. So, at last, we have proven (
5.5
).

Note that the functions
\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a)
,
\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
are continuous on
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. Thanks to (
5.5
), they have a common modulus of continuity, i.e.,

|\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a_{1})-\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a_{2})|\leq\omega(|a_{1}-a_{2}|)

(5.21)

for
a_{1},a_{2}\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
and for all
N\geq 1
, for a function
\omega(t)
satisfying

\omega(t)\rightarrow 0\;\text{as}\;t\rightarrow 0^{+}.

(5.22)

We have defined the probability measure
\mu_{\infty}
and its optimal Bayesian strategy
\sigma_{\infty}
. To complete the proof of our theorem, we must define a set
A_{\infty}\subset[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
and prove that

•

\mu_{\infty}
is supported on
A_{\infty}
,

•

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
is constant on
A_{\infty}
, and

•

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
is maximized on
A_{\infty}
over all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
.

We define
A_{\infty}
to consist of all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
such that for all
\eta>0
and all
N_{*}\geq 1
there exists
a^{0}\in A_{N}^{0}\cap(a-\eta,a+\eta)
for some
N>N_{*}
. (Recall
A_{N}^{0}
from the defining conditions for the
\mu_{N}
.)

Let us check that
\mu_{\infty}
is supported in
A_{\infty}
. Thus, let
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]\backslash A_{\infty}
. Then for some open interval
I\ni a
and some
N_{*}\geq 1
, we have
A_{N}^{0}\cap I=\emptyset
for
N>N_{*}
. Since
\mu_{N}
is supported in
A_{N}^{0}
, we have
\mu_{N}(I)=0
. Since the probability measures
\mu_{N}
converge weakly to
\mu_{\infty}
as
N\rightarrow\infty
, it follows that
\mu_{\infty}(I)=0
. So
a\notin\text{support}(\mu_{\infty})
, completing the proof that
\mu_{\infty}
is supported in
A_{\infty}
.

Next, suppose
a^{0}\in A_{\infty}
. Then there exist sequences
N_{\nu}\rightarrow\infty
and
a_{\nu}\rightarrow a^{0}
as
\nu\rightarrow\infty
, with
a_{\nu}\in A_{N_{\nu}}^{0}
. From (
5.3
) we have

\text{MR}_{N_{\nu}}-\varepsilon_{N_{\nu}}\leq\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a_{\nu})\leq\text{MR}_{N_{\nu}},

hence, thanks to (
5.21
),

\text{MR}_{N_{\nu}}-\varepsilon_{N_{\nu}}-\omega(|a_{\nu}-a^{0}|)\leq\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a^{0})\leq\text{MR}_{N_{\nu}}+\omega(|a_{\nu}-a^{0}|).

(5.23)

As
\nu\rightarrow\infty
, we have
\text{MR}_{N_{\nu}}\rightarrow\text{MR}_{\infty}
,
\varepsilon_{N_{\nu}}\rightarrow 0
, and
\omega(|a_{\nu}-a^{0}|)\rightarrow 0
thanks to (
5.22
). Therefore, (
5.23
) implies that

\lim_{N\rightarrow\infty}\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a^{0})=\text{MR}_{\infty}.

Recalling (
5.5
), we see that

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a^{0})=\text{MR}_{\infty}\;\text{for all}\;a^{0}\in A_{\infty}.

(5.24)

On the other hand, let
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
. From (
5.1
) we obtain a sequence
a_{N}\in A_{N}
(
N\geq 1
) such that
a_{N}\rightarrow a
as
N\rightarrow\infty
. Thanks to (
5.4
), we have

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a_{N})\leq\text{MR}_{N}\;\text{for each}\;N,

hence

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{N},a)\leq\text{MR}_{N}+\omega(|a_{N}-a|),

(5.25)

by (
5.21
). As
N\rightarrow\infty
, we have
\text{MR}_{N}\rightarrow\text{MR}_{\infty}
and
\omega(|a_{N}-a|)\rightarrow 0
by (
5.22
). Therefore, (
5.25
) and (
5.5
) yield the inequality

\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)\leq\text{MR}_{\infty}\;\text{for all}\;a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}].

(5.26)

From (
5.24
) and (
5.26
) we see that
\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
is constant on
A_{\infty}
, and that the maximum of
\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
over all
a\in[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]
is achieved on
A_{\infty}
. The proof of our theorem is complete.
∎

Under additional assumptions on the functions
\rho_{0}(a)
and
\rho_{1}(a)
in Section
3
, we can easily deduce that the set
A_{\infty}
in the above theorem is finite. Indeed, suppose
\rho_{0}
and
\rho_{1}
are real-analytic on
\mathbb{R}
, and suppose that for all
\varepsilon>0
we have

\rho_{0}(t)\geq-\exp(\varepsilon t)\;\text{and}\;\rho_{1}(t)\geq\exp(-\varepsilon t)\;\text{for large positive}\;t.

(5.27)

Recall that the function
a\mapsto\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
is real-analytic on
\mathbb{R}
and grows exponentially as
a\rightarrow\infty
. (See Theorem
4
.)

Under our assumptions on
\rho_{0},\rho_{1}
, it follows that the function

a\mapsto\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)=\rho_{0}(a)+\rho_{1}(a)\textsc{ECost}(\sigma_{\infty},a)

is again real-analytic on
\mathbb{R}
and exponentially large as
a\rightarrow\infty
.

In particular,
[-a_{\text{MAX}},a_{\text{MAX}}]\ni a\mapsto\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
is a nonconstant real-analytic function. Since
\textsc{Regret}(\sigma_{\infty},a)
is constant on
A_{\infty}
, it follows that
A_{\infty}
is finite, as claimed.

Combining Theorem
5
with the fact that
A_{\infty}
is finite establishes Parts (I), (II), (III) of Theorem
3
in the introduction.
